THE FOUR OF HEARTS,

MARY ANGELA DICKENS

!Author of" A Were Cypher," te.)

CHAPTER I.

"Oh, Miss Rose, what a heaven on earth our house would be if it wasn't fur the men-folk l" Sarah was drawing the curtains in her mis- tress's bedroom. She bad paused, one curtain in hand, to enunciate this sentiment, and the deep sigh that accompanied it. seemed to leave her breathless for the moment. The pause was broken by a little laugh from Miss Rose. Sarah finished drawing the curtain with a jerk of remonstrance. " I don't think it's anything to laugh at, Miss Rose," she said severely. " More especially knowing well, as I know, that you feel the same ‘bout the men as I do." "There you are perfectly right, Sarah," re- sponded Miss Rose, raising herself on her pillow, and brushing her hair back from her eyes with a sleepy gesture. "I think my own sex worth the other twice over. Ido not like men," she trans- lated, as she became awake enough to see that her words missed fire. "But you've known that for years, Sarah, and I don't quite see why you wake me this morning by stating whit we both feel so strongly." "I haven't had a wink of sleep this night for thinking on 'em in this house." "Burglars, did you think you heard ?" en- quired Miss Rose anxiously. " Burglars,—no ! I'm not one to mind a bur- glar, Miss Rose. I never was timid from a child. I'd go and meet him if I beard him, that's all ! Besides, a burglar, he's there and he's gone, as you may say. Burglars don't stay in a lady's house no longer than they can help. No. It's them two men as you had the letter from last night that's been wearing me to a thread-paper this last twelve hours! The force of imagination was stryng in Sarah. No unimaginative soul could have mentioned a thread-paper in connection with the robust com- fortable looking figure, rosy cheeks and bright eyes that she owned. Sarah was aware that she possessed certain personal attractions. She had been heard to describe herself„as " a personable woman for my years "—that mysterious adjective implying everything that a woman of forty-nine need boast. "The letter I had by the evening post about tenants for the house for August ?" "That—in course," replied Sarah. She held at this moment a brush in one hand, a comb in the other; instead of placing them on the dress- ing table she made a gesture of both hands to which they added a strange force. The gesture was one of complete repudiation. "To think," she went on, "of you, Miss Rose, letting this house as me and you have such pride in and kept ourselves to ourselves in for six years, to two men. Two men !" she repeated, laying the brush down with a bang of unutter- able disdain.

Miss Rose regarded her handmaiden seriously. " I hoped," she said, "as I told you lest night, Sarah, that you would think reasonably of it this morning. I really think you will if you reflect that it is not a matter that concerns either you or me 80 nearly as—" "sot concern me f" said Sarah. "A man in my kitchen—" She ground under her foot a stray hairpin as some slight expression of her feelings. "What I meant to say," continued Miss Rose, "was that you and I personally need hardly come in contact with them at all. You will be staying with your niece, and I shall go to Lady Manion at Longmeads for August. We never come back until our tenants are gone." "To fiud the place out o' windows !" moaned Sarah. " Well, if that is what most distresses you you could easily keep an eye on the kitchen affairs, Sarah. You will be close at hand, you see, with your niece. I cannot see that the trouble is so hopeless, I must say." " If they could only a' been ladies, like other years." " I would far rather have had ladies, but you know as 'well as I do, Sarah, that I am not justified in refusing a good tenant. I have not finally decided to let the house to Captain Sandys, but I warn you that I think it probable that I shall do so ; so it would, be wise of you to con- sider the bright side of things a little more. And now, don't you think it would be as well to give a look to Dulcinea's doings with the breakfast, and let me get up ?" Sarah crossed the pretty bedroom and shut the boor with a foreboding firmness. A strong desire to bang it was only thwarted by the influence of her long training in what she called " good ser- vice." " Bright side o' things !" she muttered, as she went downstairs, " a deal o' bright side there is about this affair. Miss Rose has wonderful eye-+ if she can see any ! I don't, and what's more I never shall !" With this hopeful prognostication she ()pence:, the door of the pleasant kitchen. " Dulcinea," she exclaimed in a distinctly hortatory tone. " What are you doing ?" She threw her voice in the direction of the pan- try, and an answer came thence in the shape of the clattering of cups and saucers. Having thus satisfied herself as to the doings of her underling, Sarah seized an armful of snowy damask and de- parted to lay the cloth for breakfast. Miss Rose Trevor, and her servant, Sarah Brown, the inhabitants of the Cottage, were both decidedly " old maids " in the eyes of the dwel- lers in the village of Southleigh. Miss Trevor was thirty-seven, and though she was still a pretty woman, with a very charming and gentle manner, she "looked her age," as the phrase is. Left quite alone in the world at thirty-one, she had settled in the village of Southleigh, where she had discovered a house exactly to her mind. With her had come, as cook and housekeeper, her old friend and servant of many years' standing— 'Sarah ' Brown. Sarah had been housekeeper to Miss Trevor's mother, and nothing would have in- duced her ever to leave the mistress she still some- times considered as a young and thoughtless girl. If any further link between the two had been needed, it would have been found in the feelings with which they both regarded the other sex. Sarah's feelings for men took their rise in con- tempt, pure and simple. To her, men were sim- ply brainless, inconvenient animals, with an un- limited capacity for "making work." And as such she despised and:detested them one and all. Miss Rose's feelings were at the other end of the same gamut. She was shy and ill at ease with men. She could not understand them. She was one of those women who have never seen a man for whom they could care, a man through whom the sex becomes comprehensible. The result was the same as in Sarah's case. She shrank from all unnecessary contact with men, and she had al- ways done so. The letter which was lending such force to Sarah's arm and speech this morning had certain- ly come with a shock into her secluded life. Miss Trevor was not at all rich, and it was her custom to add to her slender income by letting her pretty house furnished for August. Southleigh was near the coast, and it was moreover close to some ex- cellent trout streams. Both these facts were stated in the unassuming advertisement sent to a reli- able paper by Miss Trevor's reliable lawyer. The first had brought tenants for the past five Au- gusts of her ownership. Maiden or widowed ladies of unimpeachable dignity, with servants to match, had presided over The Cottage for a month and had handed it hack in faultless order to Miss Rose and Sarah. This year, however, it was the latter advantage which had called forth an answer. Miss Rose's lotter of the previous night had come from a Captain Sandys —retired from the navy. as he stated in his modest applica- tion. He desired to take The Cottage for Au- gust, and he further &sine to bring with him his own servant—" formerly a seaman, now in my service for many years." the letter said. This man, by name John Croke, would, it appeared, cook and do all that his master required, with the aid of " aboy to give daily help, who might possibly bo found locally." There were no condi- tions, no enquiries sa..l) as emanated from fensin- line would-be tenants as to soil, drainage, society, and churches. Captain Sitmlys's wants were, Plainly, few 11112 d twee thought It over au day. She did not desire a man as tenant. But on the other hand she did desire a tenant of 1.01110 kind, and July was half gone and this was the first offer she had received. This and the thought that as she said to herself, she '• need see really nothing of him," decided her. " have eonsidered it carefully," she 'told Sarah at tea-time, "and I have told Captain Sandys that lie may come. You see," she added, as she saw Sarah preparing a forlorn hope of protest, " a tenant is really so important to me, and we can put away all the things a man would spoil." The last sentence sent Sarah hurrying to the kitchen to begin then and there a process which left it, we...lt—adapted onlj. to the most primitive iageds,-%

CILAPTER IL

Sarah was ratner bored. " Tired o' doing no- thing "is the way in which she described her Three days before Captain Sandys with his ser- vant had arrived and had taken possession of The Cottage, Miss Rose had gone to her friend Lady illardon at Longmeads, a country house three miles from Southleigh. She tad set off with her neat dress-trunk at least six hours before Cap- tain Sandys could possibly arrive. " I want—and so does Sarah—to be well out of the way before they come," Miss Rose had said to a Southleigh friend. So Sarah at the same hour had betaken herself to the house of her mar- ried niece in the village, with Gus wheeling her box on a wheelbarrow before her. Gus or Aug- astus—he was known by either name a.s Sarah's temper dictated—was the " boy " at The Cot- tage. He had now been engaged by Captain Sandys, on Miss Trevor's recommendation, as the " daily help" to John Croke. Dulcinea bad gone home to her mother the day before. Thus The Cottage had been left empty for its new ten- ants, with Giza to do the honours of the back premises. Since then nothing had been beard or seen of the newcomers—nothing that is worthy of inter- est. But this was not for want of effort. Sarah had suborned men—in other words she had pro- mised her niece's eldest boy Tommy twopence to waylay Gus on his way home and to bring him to the residence of Mrs. Charles Brown. Tommy Brown had done as his great aunt bade him and Gus duly appeared in Sarah's niece's kitchen. " Well, Gus," Sarah had eagerly demanded. " What are they like ?" Dunno," was the imperturbable response. Possibly Gus thought it a moment for repayment of past injuries. "What's the man-servant like i" said Sarah sharply. "You've seen plenty of him—must

" Lame a bit," said Gus. " Whistles a bit, too."

"So does a blackbird !" retorted Sarah. " You never had no sense, Augustus. What have you been a-doing for him, anyway F" " Same as I do for you evenin's," replied Gus. " Coal an' that." " Didn't be say anything ?" "He arsk me if there wasn't no kettles nor saucepans, more nor one o' each, and I says—" "You says—" " I says there was plenty on 'ern somewhere. They was there when you was about." - . _ _ _ _ _ _ _

Al this crisis Saral had intimated unmistake- ably her desire that the interview should close, following this later by an assertion to Tommy Brown that it was not worth twopence ; at which Tommy used language strong for his tender years, and was chastised for the same by his mother.

Subsequently the milkman and the baker had been approached. The former had asserted that Captain Sandys was "not a bad sort of gent," the latter that John Croke was "one of your joking ones "—a statement which might mean anything, as indeed might the description of his master. Beyond these meagre crunibm Sarah had heard nothing, and on this, the third afternoon, the lack of information was beginning to oppress her painfully. She was sitting alone in her niece's kitchen— her niece had 'stepped out" leaving Aunt Sarah in charge of Tommy's youngest brother for half an hour. The age of Tommy's youngest brother was five months, and as he slept in his cradle the sleep of the just, he did not, strictly speaking, pro- vide his great aunt with society. Sarah had in her hands some of the sewing she "never had a min- nit to do " in her daily life, but it fell readily enough from her fingers as a knock came to the door. Anything was better than this dulness, she thought, so she rose quickly and opened the back door. Outside, in the path between two neat rows of scarlet runners, stood a man. _ _

He was, roughly speaking, about fifty-five. His face was " weathered," to use an expressive word which combines bronze and red; he had grizzled but still curly hair and very bright blue eyes. He was very neat, and held himself so up- right that one did not immediately see that he was slightly lame, one leg being drawn up a little. _ _ _

"Fell down a companion," he said very cheer- fully, catching Sarah's eye fixed on it, " always explain that first : saves trouble." Sarah felt for the first time for many years ex- tremely apologetic. This made her face quite meek and therefore more pleasing than usual as he asked equally cheerily "Name o' Mrs. Sarah Brown live here 'i"

" I'm Sarah Brown," said the owner of the name : " at your service," she added with un- usual politeness. " Obliged, I'm sure. Could I have a word with you P" Sarah opened the door wider. There was no stronger passion in her breast than curiosity at any time, and it was beginning to dawn on her that this unexpected visitor was none other than the man-servant from The Cottage, the very indi- yidua of whose doings she was so anxious to learn. So she bade him enter, cordially. "Name o' Croke myself," he said as he did so. "Tenants o' your mistress as I understand her to be." " And rightly," said Sarah quite graciously. There was something in those bright eyes of his which, unconsciously to herself, was softening Sarah's usual asperity curiously. "I called," he said taking the chair Sarah of- fered him, "about a matter o' furnishing up at your house." "Furnishing !" Sarah bristled all :over. "If that's it, it's for your master to see my mistress. He'd better do that." _

"He may a' done so by this time for all I know," Croke said calmly. "He's gone calling over to Longmeads, where I understand your mistress is a-staying—old friends Sir John Mar- don an' him is—but that's neither here nor there. He ain't a-going to talk to her about famishing not even if he do see her—reason why and it's a good 'un—he don't know." "Don't know what ?" "My business wi' you. It is wi' you, as I be- lieve the kitchen's your department when you are in your own house. ' Now, I ask yon—can anyone, bavin' a particular master, bless him for a good 'un such as few have at the same time, but I ask you can anyone fry, boil, stew, to say nothing o' vegetables, in nothing but one sauce- pan? I put it to you, Mrs. Brown—" " I've never been such a fool as to be Mrs. anything," put in Sarah sharply. " Miss Brown, I beg your pardon, I'm pure. But you take my meaning—as to the milk alone."

"Saucepans is to be had," said Sarah shortly. " Exactly so," responded Croke, very suavely. " And I'll take my oath you never sent up them little dinners of yours—which I can tell from see- ing you what they was like—with one saucepan and never a stew nor frying-pan. I says to my- self, yesterday, I says, this here's my stupidity : there's saucepans enough in some cupboard some- where if I'd the sem.: to find them. And the boy, " He's a idiot !" broke in Sarah. " Common failin' o' boys—we find it at sea, I assure you But he seemed to think there was more—somewhere mislaid. And to myself I thinks I'll go and ask the lady myself. She'll help a poor fellow out of a difficulty when it's put to her—such a pleasant lady as I've found her tOo !"

, For the first time for years Sarah's " person- able " countenance wag mantled with a colour not of anger. She sat I;ngering her sewing and . looking down at it. There was a pause which , Croke made no a hristl;.

"Best thing 'ud bey" she. said at length. " if I was to put on my bonnet and come along up and have a look for them saucepans." She was fin- gering a key in her ample pocket as she spoke. "Who knows, I might find 'em. I know the places,. you See." -

, "Think you kindly, I'm sure," Croke ejacu- lated. " I take it very kind, that I do." rive minutes later, Mrs. Charles Brown, hurry- ing home to get tea for her aunt and herself, wa-, amazed to see the former standing at the lack door with her bonnet on in amicable conversation with a man.

"I'm just stepping up to The Cottage," an- nounced Sarah airily : " I shall be back to tea " Unless you'll condescend to take a cup in your own kitchen, along o' me, ?aim Brown," ut in Croke.

It was the second of September. Miss Trevot I'd driven back to The Cottage, the won iii of her sojouna at Lougmeacis being over, and she was standing in her own drawing-room. But she was not alone. Confronting her was a man. He was tall, with one of those expressive faees tlrit ale better 'coking in later than in early life. Captain &mays bad been a frank-fsecd, good-natured hid, and there was still about hint an indefinable re- membrance of that frank-faced boy. But his fifty years had brought a dignity, a hich, com- bined ;with the candour and fearlessness, gave his whole personality a great charm. His manner was very simple, and the suggestion of his boy. hood was completed by the way in which be twisted his straw hat in his strong and capable hands.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
As Uaptarn Sandy's stood there looking aroma' him with a certain air of regretful leavetaking of the room and its associations, he scarcely realised, perhaps, how little of the past month he had ac- tually spent here, and how much of it he had spent at Lorigheads. His first call at Longmeads—that of which Croke had told Sarah —bad been paid in all un- consciousness of Miss Trevor's presence there. He had simply gone to " look up" his old friend, Sir John Mardon. But after that first visit he had constantly bicycled to Longmeads ; in fact, as has been said, he had certainly spent more time there than at The Cottage. " I'm really most awfully Sorry—no-one, in fact, was ever sorrier to leave a place than I am to leave this," he declared emphatically. " I've never—no, I've never had such a month. I wish it were all to come again. I know you, Miss Trevor, must be feeling the very reverse—only too glad to get back to the delightful house you have allowed me to occupy." He paused a moment as much for want of breath as for anything else. "I am sorry. Miss Bose put in very gently. " Sorry I" he repeated impetuously as if strin-k by her words. " Sorry ? Sorry to get back or sorry we are going? Sorry ! Do you mean is it possible that—could a charming woman like you have an idea of what a battered old sailor— in short—" _

He suddenly caught both her hands in his. Miss Rose did not draw them away. " I'd try to make you happy—on my life- I would !" be said fervently. " I've been lonely all my life—could you take pity on me ?" Miss Rose's pretty, slightly worn face looked as it must have looked at nineteen. She lifted it shyly to his. " I have never before cared for anyone —likv this—" she said. " But if it will make you happy—l shall be very happy," she ended sim- ply. COLLIERY DISASTER AT BOLD.

FIVE LIVES LOST.

A shaft accident by which five lives were lost and thirteen men and boys more or less injured occurred early on Monday morning at the Bold Colliery, half way between St. Helens and Warrington. A few minutes before six a cage containing 18 persons was sent down the shaft. The winding was controlled in the engine- house by a man named James Fowler. The cage should have stopped at a mouthing 1,650 ft. below the surface. Here there were strong cross-beams upon which tho cage should have rested. From some cause which has yet to be thoroughly cleared up the cage did not stop. It struck the timber work with such force as to crash through it, the occupants of the cage being thrown violently about. For another 90ft. the cage descended, and then it struck and was stopped I by a strong platform. This had been erected in the shaft in connection with some sinking operations. Had it not been there it is to be feared that the cage would have fallen into the sump hole 150 ft. below and all the 18 occupants been drowned. Within the cage the effects of the crash were terrible. The occupants seem to have been hurled up to the roof and thrown one against another in a heap. All were bruised and cut, most of them badly crushed, and some had bones broken. When a rescue party could get to work it was found that four of the 18 were dead, and a fifth died shortly after reaching the hospital. One had been thrown out of the cage, and was with difficulty extricated from his position. The work of rescue was rendered easier by the fact that just above the platform there is an airway giving access to another shaft. Through this the injured and the dead were conveyed, the former to the St. Helens Hospital, the latter to the Clock Face Inn. Doctors were soon in attendance at the pit to give first aid. I The following is a complete list of the casualties : I KILLED. James M'llenry (14 years), Coral Terrace, Collins I Green.

Evan Davies, (age not known), Francis-street,Sutton. John Caveney, Mercer-street, Burtonwood, died an hour and a half after admission to hospital. INJURED.

GOOD NEWS FOR CYCLISTS.

The liability to side-slip upon greasy roads, over- watered tram-lines and similar surfaces, has long been recognised as a very serious drawback to bicycling,rnore I particularly in thickly populated districts. The evil is unfortunately a growing one, for tramways and light railways are being extended daily. The Cyclists' Tour- ing Club is about to make a serious effort to combat the difficulty, to which end it offers "a prize or prizes to the value of two hundred guineas, for the best means of preventing side-slip on bicycles as at present in general use." The conditions laid down provide:— (a) That all entries, accompanied by f all particulars and drawings, must be sent to the Cyclists' Tour- ing Club, 47, Victoria-street, London, S.W., not later than the 31st March, 1905. (b) Thatan entrance fee of one guinea, which will not be returnable, must accompany each device. (c) That the Judges shall be appointed by the Council of the Cyclists' Touring Club, and their decision shall be final. 1 That the Cyclists' Touring Club shall make no claim to any patent right or property in any of the devices submitted. Forms of entry may be obtained from the Secretary of the Club at the address above-mentioned.

• At an inquest held on Wednesday at the Whitechapel Infirmary on a woman of about 40, who died from pneumonia and heart disease accelerated by the rough life she had led, it was stated that the only name she was known by was" Maudie." She had no friends, and no one knew how she got a living.

The Coroner, addressing the jury said : You quite understand I have only come down here to-night in order that I may take formal evidence as to the deaths of these poor fellows so as to enable the relatives to carry out their burial. It will be absolutely necessary to have an adjournment of this inquiry to some future time and place, so that the whole of the circumstances may be inquired into, and so that we may have present Mr. Hall, his Majesty's Inspector of Mines, and the various interests represented, and more especially that the engine winder may be present with, I hope, some legal representative. I won't trouble you with many details. You have, I am sure, seen sufficient in the newspapers to enable you to know what you are here to inquire about. Evidence of identification was given by John Swift, of 69, Ramford-street, Parr, St. Helens (who identified his son John) ' • Caroline Williams (Evan Davies, her cousin); Isaac Rothwell,l9, Fairclough•street, Burton- wood (his son Thomas); Patrick M'Henry, Choral- terrace, Collins Green (John M'Henry) ; and John Caveney, 83, Bercer-street, Burtonwood (his son John). Miss Oates, matron of the St. Helens Hospital, said Caveney was admitted at half-past seven in the morning, and died at nine o'clock.

The Coroner said he thought thelfull inquiry ought to be held at St. Helens Town Hall because there would be a great many people there representing various interests. The engine winder ought also to be repre- sented, at least he would be very foolish if he was not. Friday, the 27th inst., at 9.30 a.m., was thereupon agreed to, and the coroner said he would arrange for a , conveyance to take the jury into St. Helens and back. I Mr. Peace said that, on behalf of the colliery company, he would like to associate himself with the expressions of sympathy the coroner had so feelingly made, and to state how much the colliery company regretted that most lamentable accident, and how much they felt for and sympathised with the families and relatives of the poor men in their bereavement; and, on their behalf, he was able to promise that anything they could do to facilitate or elucidate the business of the inquiry would be done by the company. With regard to the p.or sufferers in the hospital, the company was anxious that anything they could do to add to their comfort should I be done.

Mr. Riley thanked the coroner and Mr. Peace for their kind expressions in regard to his clients.

WANTED A CHANCE.

George Gallagher, a convicted thief, for depredation at Liverpool and Widnes, pleaded hard before the Widnes bench on Monday, for another chance to enable him to obtain an honest living. To a charge of robbing George Stubbs of his watch and chain, value 10s., he pleaded that he had been over-indulgent during the Christmas holidays, and they must have been having a "deal in watches." Both had been drinking together at the Black Horse, Farnworth, where prisoner sold his chain and offered his watch. When outside prisoner embraced Stubbs somewhat lovingly, snatched his watch, and decaruped.—Police-constable Davidson effected his arrest and was complimented by the pri- soner on the capture. Prisoner got three months' hard labour.

Mr. Brighouse's "Coming of Age." In presiding over an inquest at Bootle on Monday, Mr. S. Brighouse (county coroner for South-West Lancashire) completed twenty-one years' service in that capacity. The occasion was taken by the jury and police to congratulate the cot oner upon the fact, and to refer to his invariable courtesy In acknowledging the compliments, Mr. Brighouse said he had endeavoured to do his duty to the relatives of the deceased and to the employers of those who met their deaths through accident. He reminded them that so long as they had carried out their duties in such a manner as to satisfy their cons- cience, he thought they could stand criticism from the outside world. Referring to the recent discussion anent the abolition of juries, he said if it was not for the invaluable evperience and knowledge possessed by many of the jurymen of several commercial pursuits he would not be able to arrive at a proper conclusion at the inquiries held by him. The juries of South- West Lancashire were of great assistance to him, and he considered it would not be at all pleasant when a coroner would have to preside over a court without a jury to help him. For his own part, he had no fault to find with the verdicts returned by his juries, and he appreciated the manner in which the gentlemen I called upon by the police to serve responded to the summons. • (Applause).

WARRINGTON CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

SUCCESSFUL YEAR'S TRADING. ---- 300 Guineas Testimonal to the Secretary.

A successful year's trading WAi under review at the forty fifth annual meeting of the Warrington Co-opera- tive Society held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey street,on :Monday evening. Considering the bitterly cold weather there was a satisfactory attendance, Mr. T. Birchall presiding. There were also present :—Messrs. F. Monks, W. Jeffery and A. H. Rathbone (managing secretary.)

===== 0002601_19050121_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESIDENT'S ADDRESS.

The CIIMEMAN said that was, he believed, his 39th opportunity of presiding at annual meetings of the Society—(applause)—and he was once more in the position of being able to congratulate the Society most heartily on a very successful year and a record quarter. The sales during last quarter amounted to considerably over £3,000 above the previous period.ll they called to mind that the times were bad and work had been short, many people having nothing to do, this was a really wonderful result, and it showed that even ' bad times were a stimulant to thinking workingmen to see how best they could make their means go as far as possible. During the past quarter they had paid £l3O more in dividend than in the previous quarter making a total of £6,318 195.4 d, He could say that in all the 39 times he had presided at those meet- ings there had never been one of them in which the out- look had been so satisfactory as the present one. From the reports to hand,

THE PROGRESS OF CO-OPERATION had been very good, but it was not what it ought to be. When they thought what a society like.that offered to workingmen, he was astonished that they did not take advantage of it. Let them look at the Stock Exchanges at 6businesses paying 10 per cent. ; they were charged double:the premium for the privilege of entering them. For more than 25 years, the Society had been paying 15 per cent., not on money invested but on the spendings—on theamount they had got their equivalent for—besides 10d.in the £ for money invested. That Society was the most democratic institution he. , had ever known. Firms doing good business asked enormous amounts for their shares, while in the Co-operative Society each individual in the community was only asked the same terms as when the Society was struggling for existence, more than 40 years ago. He was glad to tell the members that they were going on successfully. Their Winwick-street branch new shops were just completed, and they were now fitting them. Every branch was doing satisfactory business, and they had not one failure. (Hear, hear.) i They were hoping to take steps to branch out n new directions by and by. The whole Society was working most harmoniously. They had an excellent committee, and at the last annual meeting they put on men in the early prime of life—business men who were trying to work harmoniously with two or three older ones who kept the ballast. (Laughter and hear, hear.) He did not think there was any danger of their going wrong.

PROPOSED NEW CENTRAL PRENIfSES. A CAFE SUGGESTED.

It was reported by the SECRETARY that there were 150 additional members as compared with the previous quarter. The PRESIDENT said it had been thought desirable to rebuild the present central buildings and put a handsome suite of rooms for grocery below. Of course that was only yet under consideration. There was not nearly room enough for the business at the present time and therefore it was very desirable to extend. Mr. NIGHTINGALE wished they could get the work- ingmen to be more interested in the Co-operative Stores. The Society ought to bring itself much more promin- ently before the public. He would like a large tea meeting and a meeting afterwards at which there would be some prominent speakers. Mr. ENGLAND remarked that he was cycling to Southl port a short time ago, and when going through St. Helens he had refreshment at the Co-operative Stores in that town. He thought that they in Warrington should establish a co-operative cafri like St. Helens. He of course did not suggest such an elaborate one as St. Helens, but he thought that a reasonable scheme could be formed.

The PRESIDENT : We have not room for our business requirements alone. nany people who come into our shop cannot wait. We want more room to do our busi- nels and to employ more men. One of our branches has actually done more business than the Central. A RECORD QUARTER.

The committee presented the following report :—We have pleasure in submitting for your approval and adoption the 170th quarterly report and balance sheet for the quarter ending December 31st 1901. The sales for the quarter amount to £37,229 ss. 11d., and are the highest ever recorded, showing an increase of £3,382 Os. 103. over last quarter and £1,012 11s. 73. over the corresponding quarter of last year, each branch contri- buting to the increase. The profits, after providing for interest on capital, depreciation, and all liabilities, will allow the usual dividend of 3s. in the £on members' purchases, with .C 165 139. 3d to be carried forward. We note with pleasure the con- tinued increase in the sales at Winwick-street branch, and hoped to be ready in a short time to separate the provision department from the grocery ; this will give considerably more room and enable us to do an extended business in each department. We are pleased to inform the members that a lady assistant has been appointed to take charge of the mantle and ladies' underclothing department at the Sankey-street drapery establishment. We have secured a choice selection of goods, and ask for an inspection, knowing that they are of a quality and style suited to all tastes. Mr. W. JEFFERY moved and i%fr. JOHNSON seconded the adoption of the report, which was unanimodsly agreed to.

The PRESIDENT said that at the last quarterly meet- ing, the committee were instructed to take into con- sideration the past services of the secretary. He had thought of the history of the past 40 years which carried I him back to the time when they were struggling through the infancy of the Society. They had appointed shop- man after shopman, but somehow, things did not go along satisfactorily. At length, at one committee meeting, they were considering what to do, when one gen'leman—Mr. Monks—said "Now Hornby Rath- bone, will you take it on?" Mr. Rathbone was a me cutter getting good wages then. After a little hesitation he agreed to take the position of manager at the magnificent sum of 265. a week—less than he was earning at the time. They struggled on, and at that time the Committee men also took their part in serving in the shop on Fridays and Saturdays. They knew howlthe Society increased, and they also knew the more recent history of the Society. He ask,,d them to take that into consideration and to do something handsome, because they did not want to do an ihing shabby. The Committee had passed the followium resolution on October 26th, 1904:—" Proposed in accordance with the resolution passed at the quarterly meeting on October 17th, that the Board of Manage- ment take into consideration the best means of giving expression of our appreciation of 40 years faithful services rendered to this Society by our esteemed manager, Mr. A. H. Rathbone, it is hereby resolved that we recommend to the annual meeting to be held on January 16th, that we present Mr. Rathbone a cheque for 250 guineas and an enlarged first-class photograph of him to be placed in the Board room, the above presentation to be made at a public meeting, the time and place of which to be subsequently arranged. (Applause.)" Continuing, Mr. Birchall said that Mr. Rathbone had been in the service of the Society from the prime of manhood until now, when he was 71 years of age. Mr. GEORGE WALLINGTON said he was delighted to see that the committee had recommended such a hand- some sum. Of course he was precluded from saying more.

Mr. J. JEFFERY moved and Mr. Mow seconded the 'adoption of the minutes. After a discussion, it was decided that the sum be increased to three hundred guineas. Mr. W. JEFFERY remarked that this would only amount to 10d. a member.

Mr. Rathbone, who during this discussion had been out of the room, was then called in,and the PRESIDENT,

addressing him, said he wa.s delighted to inform hito that the meeting had decided to present him with 300 guineas and an enlarged photograph. Of course, ho could give the photograph back to the Society if he liked, so that it could be hung in the Board room. :IMr. IZATFIRONE said he had not words to express to tho meeting what he thought and under the circumstances he could not say much. It certainly did look like a dream to him to look back at 44 years in their service. He could scarcely think that the time had passed over. I He hied always devoted his whole energy to the business and he owed much to Mr. Monks for his support and advice. When he became manager, the sales amounted to £4.1 a week, and now they were something over £3,000 a week. He thanked them warmly for their handsome gift. (Applause.) Mr. F. MONKS said if he had been instrumental in finding them snch a servant, as he believed he had, he hoped they might always have a succession of them. They were all most enthusiastic in those de_ys. He had carried many a sack of flour on his back. In fact, he believed that he carried the first sack of flour into the shop one Saturday morning. It was one of their ideals to establish not only a distributing shop, but ho had an idea of a manufacturing concern also.(Applause) It was a bad job, but it was the jealousy of the wire. drawers which prevented its success. He could see that it was no use beating against a windmill because the windmill would beat them, and so he started on his own account. It was quite true that from time to tune he had watched over the interests of the Society and when Mr. Rathbone wanted advice, he had always been quite welcome to it. He might say however, that Mr. Rathbone had required very little as there was no one who could have shown greater capacity and ability in the management of tha business When he said he had saved at least £30,000 by his good judgment he was quite certain he was well within the mark. In conclusion Mr. Monks asked all the members of the Society to put all their jealousies aside and elect men capable of doing the work for them. (Applause.) The meeting closed with a vote of thanks to thO hairman, on the motion of Mr. Monks.

STOCKTON HEATH.

BAND OF HOPE.—A large audience assembled to witness an excellent entertainment in connection with the Hill Cliffe and Stockton Heath United Band of Hope, held last week in the Providence Schoolroom. The Rev. J. m. Hughes presided over a large audience. The programme was provided by the members of Bold. street and the Stockton Heath Bands of Hope. The action song," Cloud and sunshine," by twelve girls, was well given ; and violin solos were rendered by Master Crompton and Mabel Tonge. The Warrington Banda Hope Juvenile Choir, under the conductorship of Mr. A. Spruce. submitted a capital rendering ef the test piece sung at the recent Eisteddfod, " Don't forget tho old folks "the blending of the children's voices and the beautiful words of the piece making it most effective, Miss May Isherwood received a well merited encore for her singing of " Killarney," whilst Miss F. Bennett was equally successful in "Three green bonnets." Master Richardson contributed "The auction," and Master Stanley Jones caused much amusement, with the character song, "Lub me, Dinah," which called forth an encore. Miss May Mather, aged four years, gave two recitations in capital style. A novel item in the pro. gramme wile a "Skipping song," which was contributed by Miss Nellie Berry whose abilities as a skipper called forth an entleasiastic encore. One of the principaf events of the evening was the rendering of the cantata "Rival Monarchs: King Alcohol v. Queen Temperance" which was very well given, the principal characters being taken by the Misses Wilson, Kershaw, Parry, Evans. Isherwood, Martin and Wood, and Mess. Whitfield, Unsworth, G. Moss, P. Moss, and Masters Walker, Parry, Kershaw and Doncaster, all of whom acquitted themselves very creditably. IWOlNffleito

Eczema is more prevalent in winter than at any other season. In the severest cases Zam-Buk has been found to afford a complete and lasting cure. "I had my first attack eig'.t years ago," says hfr. George Thompson, of Worlseley Bridge, near Stafford, 'when an eruption broke out on my hands and arms, 'which swelled and discharged a great deal. When my doctor had done all he could for me I was still very bad and became an in-patient at the Birmingham Queen's Hospital. During the first week in the hospital the eruptions discharged a great deal, and I had to lie on my back with my arms upon a rack for some time. The doctors told me I was suffering from gouty eczema (a complaint very prevalent in the damp and cold season). I improved under the treatment, but when I came out of the hospital the doctor told me the disease might break out again any time, and it did come upon me again in a mos;, horrible way. I was soon ona mass of sores front head to foot, my eyes were all made up, and my hair came off my head. " My wife asked the doctor whether he thought should ever get better, and he said : No, it would kill me; and when it went I should go.' He said he never saw anyone in such a condition. The form the disease came in was in large lumps,a kind of swelling and discoloration ; and then it discharged a lot of nasty, sticky fluid. My wife used to wash me and dress 1110 night and morning, and sponge me all over. I need not describe the agonies I went through. The diseaso brought me down very low and weak. I had all kinds of remedies, such as carbolic oil and olive oil, mixed ; I was bathed with special soap ; in fact, I tried a lot of ointments and medicine, which, however, only seemed to drive the disease in instead of killing it. 1 suffered constantly for twelve months, whets instead of having these disagreeable eruptions and diS• charges, my skin came off in scales from head to foot like the scales of .fish. In fact, my skin peeled off two or three times, and ever afterwards I had a return of this scaling every year, especially in the winter months. While I was suffering very badly I read what Zam-Buk had done for others. I thought I would give it a trial, so sent for a sample box. I then got a full supply, and noticed how the applications began to put the skin into a much healthier condition. PRACTICAL CHRISTIANITY.

Wesleyan Mission Work among the Poorer Classes.

LECTURE AT BOLD-STREET.

Among the poorer classes of the country the home missionary organization of the Wesley in Methodist Church is doing a splendid work. "Practical Christianity" best sums up its aim, for the workers have had the satisfaction of rendering help which has been sorely needed and of earning the gratitude of those who have come under their influence. With the object of enlisting increased support from Warrington Wesleyans on behalf of this excellent agency, the annual home missionary meeting in cbnnection with Bold-street Church on Tuesday evening was made the occasion of an Illustrated lecture by the Rev. Samuel Wilkes, of. Wiltshire Mission. There was a good attendance, Mr W. Silcock presiding. In presenting the annual report tie Rev. G. H. CARNSON said the income to home missions from the various circuits of the kingdom during the year 19)03-4 showed an increase of £227 3s. 4d. The ordinary income from collections, subscriptions, &c., was £2G,888 12s. 4d., being £274 14s 7d. less than the previous year. Other items of income showed an increase of £5Ol _ _

s. lid. The total receipts for the year were £37,740 3s. 7d. and the expenditure £37,950 3s. 9d., leaving a deficiency of £2lO Os. lid. This sum, added to the adverse balance due at the beginning of the year, made a total debt due to the treasurer of £383 14s. Towards the income the Warrington circuit contributed £4B 4s. 11d. The Sun- day collections at Bold-street realised £4 7s. 6d. ; the the public meeting £1 3s. Bd.; and the Juvenile Associa- tion, £6 16s. 6d. The grants made to the Liverpool district amounted to £lOO. .The CHAIRMAN said that whatever might be the difference of opinion with regard to foreign missions, there surely could be no difference as to the importance and value of home missions. The story of the work which was being done in great centres ought to fire them With enthusiasm. The Rev. S. WILKES proceeded with his lecture, pointing out that the work to which he would call their attention was the practical outcome of Christianity. The man of the world wanted to know that Christanity was good for this world,and the Christian socialism at work within their own church was setting a magnificent object lesson before the man in the street as to what the love of Christ constrained them to do. The work of the deaconesses was first illustrated, and the speaker pointed out that eighty-four of these enthusiastic lady helpers were engaged in home towns, while there were eleven in foreign lands. The magnificent Leysian Mission which is such a centre of activity and good in- fluences in London was interestingly described, and the lecturer went on to allude to the work of the Bermondsey settlement in the poorest parts of the city. The settlement idea, he said, was a very effective means of solving the problem of "How to bring the classes together,"for the rich and the poor,the educated and the illiterate were brought into contact. At Ber- mondsey splendid work of a religious, recreative, and educational character was carried on, and sunshine was I brought into the lives of those who were handicapped by unhealthy and gloomy surroundings. The outcome of this social work was that hundreds had been con- verted to God and were on the muster roll of His ' Church. The concluding part of the lecture was devoted to a plea on behalf of the workingmen. Most of his ministry, said Mr. Wilkes,had been spent among them and he had therefore some knowledge of them, of their surroundings, and the way in which they were handi- capped in life. He had also some knowledge of their aspirations and of the way in which they could be uplifted. The importance of the subject could not be over-estimated. Let them never believe that the 'Workingman was unapproachable. One of the best ways of getting at the workingman was to provide a counter attraction to the places to which he was accustomed to go. Why should not the Christian Church provide him with a club, and wholesome entertainment? Saturday night, Said the lecturer, was the "Devil's night" in all great towns but the Christian Church had not sufficiently realized that and had not laid itself out to outwit the devil on Saturday night. Mr. Wilkes gave a number of instances of the way in which he had been able to touch the hearts of the roughest men and bring them to Christianity by work:zg on the lines he had suggested. Miring the evening Miss Taylor very effectively sang "I will sing of Thy mercy" and Mr. Clayton gave "Gates of the West" in creditable style. Mr. Frank Hickman ccompanied. On the motion of Mr. BARLOW, seconded by Mr. R. IL SUTTON, a hearty vote of thanks was passed to the speakers and vocalists. -

The services on Sunday were also on behalf of home missions, the preachers being the 113 v. Joseph Manning and the Rev. J. S. Hughes. ST. HELENS.

MORE PASSIVE RESISTERS AT ST. HELENS.

At St. Helens Police Court on Monday, Joseph White- house, William Barker, and Arthur Foster, three well- known Nonconformists, appeared as passive-resisters. A long statement was made to the bench, and the chairman (Mr. T. Glover) said that however much the magistrates might be in sympathy wtth their ideas, they were only there to carry out the law, and that was all they could do. They were bound to make the order to pay. THRASHED HIS WITE WITH I WHIP.

, John Aspinall, a horse dealer, of 126, Donriding lane. St.llelens was summoned on Monday morning for persis- taut cruelty to his wife but did not appear. It was stated that the parties had been married twelve years,and during that time defendant had continually used violence to his wife without provocation. A few weeks ago he wanted to bring his mother to the house,but complainant who was an outdoor hawker, did not think she could look after the old lady, and so refused to allow her to come to the house. In consequence of this defendant went to his wife in the wa.shhouse and thrashed her with a horsewhip until (as complainant stated) he was thoroughly "fagged out." The same day he threatened her with a knife, and had it not been for a thick shawl round her neck the knife would have cut her throat. On various occassions he had given her black eyes, and on a later occasion had thrown a sugar basin at her. The bench granted a separation order. with 15s. a week, defendant also to pay £2 12s. costs.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. Another of these pleasant meetings was held on Sun- day, when there was a good attendance. Three new mernbe•s were received, and the total is now 578. Mr. H. W. Greenleaves presided, and referred to the great 'Revival in Wales, and hoped it would soon reach the town of Warrington. Mr. G. F. Worthen, of Manches- ter, was the speaker. His address on " Content and Discontent " was very interesting. Birds and animals, he said, carried out their natural instincts with apparent contentment, but when we came to the higher life of man, then the spirit of discontent was plainly visible. Man was not merely a machine, but a being, guided, impelled by ahigher Poser. There was a moral discon- tent, which was created by pride and selfishness ; on the other hand discontent was often the beginning of pro- gress and advancement. The difficulties of life were often helped by a contented spirit •, sometimes discon- tent caused idleness and stupidity, but we must all try to get the true contented spirit, possessed by Paul, and be discontented with all things that were slow for God and humanity. Hence discontent in the true spirit was commendable, because it would help us wonderfully through all trials and troubles. A letter of sympathy was sent to Mr. Francis Brown, who is one of the oldest members of the society; and a letter of condolence was also sent to Mrs. Kirkland, who recently lost her husband. Mr. William Rutter very pleasingly rendered a cornet solo," The Star of Bethlehem." Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. Mt. J. Garner and the President (the Rev. J. Yonge) also took part in the meeting.

"FAILURES IN ENGLAND AND WALES.—According to Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of Commercial failures in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, January 13th, was 158, viz.:—New bankruptcy proceedings published in the London Gazette, 812 ; deeds of arrangement registered, 76. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were: bankruptcies, 76 ; deeds of arrangement, 80—total 156 ; being an increase of 2. The total num- ber of commercial failures recorded during the 2 weeks of the present year is 323 ; the total number recorded , in the corresponding 2 weeks of last year was 290 I showing an increase of 33.

Society Revivalists.—Amongst the many ladies of title who are giving their personal services on behalf of the Torrey-Alexander Mission, is Lady Wiruborne, who has long been identified with the Evangelical party in the Chusch cf England. She founded Some time ago the Lad'es League, to combat the forces of ritualism in the Church, and it is therefore not surprising to find Lady Wimborne in the van of a Puritanic religious revival. This, however, is intended to appeal to all creeds and classes, and the Bishop of London he's expressed his approval of the Mission's programme. One of the features of its organization is the formation ' of a committee of 150 ladies, who will make house to house calls in the fashionable districts of London, with an invitation to the Albert Hall meetings. A great many of the members are women of title, who are enter- ing into the work with whole-hearted zeal. The Moody and Sarkey revival of 1874-75 was popular rather than fashionable, but it would seem probable that the Torrey Alexander mission will achieve both these distinctions I 'I he Spencer Churchills.—Lady Wimborne is the eldest of a distinguished group of daughters of the seventh Duke of Marlborough, and it is a singular rule in the laws of precedence, that enables her sister Lady Sarah Wilson to rank before her, especially as Lady Wimborne married a peer whilst Lady Sarah married a commoner. In marrying a peer however, Lady Cornelia Spencer Churchill, took her husband's rank as wife of Baron Wimborne, but Lady Sarah in marrying a commoner, retains her own rank as a daughter of a duke, which is higher than that of a baroness. Both ladies are sisters of the late Lord Randolph Churchill, and by consequence, aunts to Mr. Winston Churchill. A Remarkable Woman.—The fiction of a modern romance finds its counterpart in the life of Louise Michel, the fiery Anarchist, and tender sympathiser with suffering. No doubt she was the creature of environment, and, like many others was impelled to war against Society by the circumstances of her birth. She knew that it was not her fault, that a stigma was cast upon her, and, rebelling against the injustice, was guilty in the Commune of Acts, which, but for her sox, would probably have caused her to be shot, when order was restored. In other respects her character was beyond reproach and in ministering to the wounded she I was an angel of mercy. A character dominated by such contradictory influences. ern only be explained by some degree of insanity, the exact extent of which it would be difficult to assess. But then it is said that we are all mad, more or less. I Ribbons at the Sales.—Those women who have secured lengths of ribbons at the sales,—and in this department the bargains" have been exceptionally good—will have[little difficulty in finding uses for them. A novelty is the ribbon feather, which is first fashioned' of wire, narrow ribbon being then rucked all over it. The effect , is that of the closely set breast feathers of a bird. These ribbon feathers are finding a place in much of the useful walking and travelling headgeas, and the home worker will find it a delightful and inexpensive idea,for brighteninr, up a hat or toque that begins to look middle aged. Then there are the belts and neckwe sr, for which ribbons of every width and kind may be utilised, dainty blouses, vests, and fronts, which look especially well,built of alternate lengths of iibbons,and net or lace. Another use to which ribbons may be put is the tiny formal bows, so much in favour for trim- ming the gathered old world gown. In the matter of ribbons,one can hardly go wrong at any of ;the Season's sales and the same may be said of goon lace. Up-to-date Veils.—Veilings are much worn at the moment, and until March hss come and gone, with its cutting ' complexion-destroying "winds, they promise to remain in favour. But the veil though it may save the majority of us something in the way of creams and lotions is an expersive luxury. To commence with, it must in every instance match the hat or toque with which it is worn, and needless to say the tinted varieties are far more costly than the black and white specimens we have had with us so long. Some of the colourings in veils are not happy, nor are they We. For example the bright purple and bright green varieties may be" smart" when worn with headgear of the same shade, but they make the wearers look hideous, and i there s danger that the colouring may injure the skin. A rash on the face, nose included, brought about by the dangeous dye has been experienced by several of the devotees of fashion, and like highly coloured hosiery, and gloves, highly coloured veils should be avoided. A soft clear white veiling is the best possible We LT, and this should be gathered round the hat add also under the chin.

_ The Coiffure.—There is no excuse for wearing the hair dowdily in these days, when there are so resny pretty st3,les of the coiffure in vogue. At the moment the waved rolled back pompadour, with a stray curl or two falling on the forehead is fashionable, but there seems to be a decided tendency to return to the fringe which became so much vulgarised in former days. The hair is less puffed out above the ears than it was, and in many coiffures is now taken up comparatively tightly, and arranged in two puffs across the head, one of which falls over the brow and closely resembles the old- fashioned curled fringe. The wearing of many jewelled or ornamental combs and pins in the hair has be,n over- ' done, and is no longer in favour with the cultured classes. Glittering butterflies with spangled or iridescent beetle's wipgs, are left for those who study effect rathe- than refinement, and this welcome change has been hastened by the fact that the overloading of the hair with orna- ments tends to make the wearer look older. CONCERNING HAI 8.

Already there are rumours abroad concerning the straw hats to be worn during the coining spring, says the Tatter. The straight, fiat brim will be in a great measure superseded by one of undulating form. Crowns will be of medium height, and decidedly smaller at the top than at the base. As regards the newest form of sailor hat, it has a large but not a particularly high crown, the brim being slightly turned up all round; and it is also under- stood that while the toque will remain fashionable it will be oval rather than round. The hats worn in the Rue de la Pah( are certainly smaller than those seen in Bond-street, says M.A.P. ; they are turned sharply up at one side, and poised at an acute angle on the side of the head. Veils have returned to favour, and are drawn closely under the chin. They often match the hat in colour, especially in Crown, dark blue, and violet. Beaver felt is much worn, in black, white, grey, and brown. Paris must be previous, and spring hats have already appeared. These are in straw and taffeta t silk, wreathed with flowers—roses, violets. and mimosa.

TO-DAY ' 9 RECIPE.

DEVILLED Wooncoca.—Three-parts roast a brace of woodcock, split the heads, and cut the birds up into neat little joints, dusting each freely with the following mixture: Sift together equal parts of fine salt, cayenne, and curry powder with double the amount of mushroom powder, and, when well blended, use. Bruise the brains and the trail with the yolk of a hard-boiled egg, a dash of pounded mace, the very finely-grated rind of half a small lemon, and half a spoonful of soy. When these are all smoothly blended, stir in a tablespoonful of mushroom ketchup, a sherry glaslful of Madeira, and the juice of two Seville oranges. Put this in the chafing-dish and boil it well up, then lay in the cut-up bird, &c., watch it all just re-boil; now turn down the lamp, cover down the pan, and keep it all just simmering until most of the gravy is absorbed. It should be stirred occasionally during the process. When you consider it is sufficiently saturated, sprinkle it with a small amount of the very best salad oil, stir it all well tither, and serve at once.

THE DETERIORATION OF BRITISH HEALTH AND PHYSIQUE,

THE EARL OF MEATH.

1. Every effort should be made to counteract 1 all unnecessary increase of towns, and, when 1 possible, new factories or industries should be established in, and old ones transferred to the country. 2. Care should be taken that all towns should De furnished with a sufficient number of parks, and especially a ith a large number of small play- grounds, fitted with gymnastic apparatus, and under the charge of caretakers, and so placed that no workman's house should if possible be more than quarter of a mile from such a ground. 3. Children should be taught the rudiment: of hygiene, sanitation, and physiology, so as tc have some slight knowledge of the causes of di- sease, and the means which can be taken tc avoid it. 4. They should, by cookery classes in school: be shown how to obtain the largest amount of nourishment with he smallest expenditure on food ; what kind of foods are wholesome; and how food should be cooked so as to make it not only palatable but easily digested and conse- quently nourishing. 5. They should be tatght self-restraint in order to learn when they grow up how to rule themselves, and how to place a bridle on their appetites and passions, and on those indulgences which are harmful to health. G. The muscles and lungs of the children and their general bearing should be improved by physical exercise and drill, and it should be the duty of the educational authorities to see that, whilst attending school, the children are proper- ly fed. The authorities should be empowered also to bring the parents to account if they neg- lect their natural duties. 7. The girls should be taught by housewifery classes how to make a home clean, wholesome,

bright and attractive. and as so Many have tc; act as nurses from their earliest years, the pro- per way of feeding and tending infants. The ignorance of many married women has wrecked before now the physical welfare of their off- spring. It is not an uncommon thing to bear of a young mother amongst the poorer classes giving beer, whisky, and gin to infants, and feeding them on the same food as she takes herself, long before the proper time has arrived for weaning the children, with the result that a quite unneces- sarily large percentage of infants die before they are three years of age. If the home were made more attractive, and the food more palatable and nutritious, ths men would not be tempted to leave it so frequently for the public-house. It cannot be too widely known that there is no nourishment in stimulants, that raw spirits des- troy the digestion, and that a man who desires to retail his health will do well either to entirely abstain from stimulants, or to take them only at meals, and then with the most rigid moderation. All these thnigs should be taught, and they would be of more real value to the children than many lessons they now learn, and which are promptly forgotten as soon as they leave school. THE WORKING MAN'S TART.

ta,'

. _ •

Amongst earthly blessings there is none greater than the possession of physical health , and strength, but how few there are that give serious thought to the subject. As soon GS we , become Conscious that the wheels cf the machi- nery of life are running heavily, generally I through our own negligence, we rush to the pro- fessional repairer, or even to the advertising quack, and are willing to pay heavily for assist- ance which can only be temporary, if we neg- lect to alter the causes which have occasioned the evil. How much wiser would it be, if, indi- vidually and collectively, we would make a study of the laws which lead to health or disease, and, as far as circumstances permit, would regulate oar lives in accordance with them? As a nation we insist, and rightly insist, that All children shall be educated. We draw up codes of instruction; we appoint and pay schools masters and schoAmistresses to impart thi- knowledge ; we spend thousands of pounds in order that every child, however poor, may be given an equal chance in life ; and yet, how s.mall-a part of all this thought, trouble and ex- penditure is directed towards imparting to chil- dren knowledge, which, in after years shall en- able them to understand the laws of nature and to maintain their own and their future chil- dren's bodies in health and strength. MORE KNOWLEDGE—LESS LEARNING. What is the good of book learning if we are ignorant of the nature of our own bodies and ot tkise laws which were intended to make life ..appy, but if neglected made it a burden and a misery? What avails material prosperity, if either by our own fault, or that of our parents, we are un- able, through ill-health, either to enjoy life or to be of use to our country or our friends Let us consider what are the subjects, a know- ledge of which lead if properly applied, to an increase in physical health. Surely such as hygiene, sanitation, physiology, dietetics, cookery and housewifery. Of course 1 it would be foolish to attempt to teach children more than the mere elements of some of these subjects, but the general principles which un- derlie the preservation of health might well oc- cupy the attention of teachers and scholars for a reasonable number of hours in each year. If we look through the branches .of knowledge studied by our children at school, how seldom do we come across any subjects which may be of use to them in after years in assisting them to preserve their bodies in health, and when these subjects are taught, how thinly attended are the classes ? Why is this ? It is due in a large measure to lack of teachers qualified to instruct in these matters, to inca- pacity at headquarters to understand the real needs of the people, and in no small degree on the part of the parents to a misapprehension of the present conditions of life. Parents are apt to argue, that because they never learnt these subjects, which in plain Eng- lish only mean the study of how best to keep the body in health, it is unnecessary for then children to do so. They forget, however, that the most rapid transformation in the character of the British people is taking place before their eyes, that the England of their youth is not the England we know, and that the England of ten twenty and of thirty years- hence will be en- tirely different from the England of to-day. The fact is, that the British, from having been main- ly an agricultural, are rapidly becoming an ur- ban people' In other words, whereas formerly they lived in the country, now they live in the towns. The rapidity with which this change is taking place is hardly realised by most people. Health is not so jeopardised in the country as It is in towns, and hence men and women, who have • to lire in cities should study more care fully how to preserve their health. In 1801 the Whole population of England, Wales and Scot- land was only 10,942,000, whereas now it is 36,998,181, toad this addition is almost entirely urban. Twenty-five years ago only about, half the population lived in urban districts, now the proportion is threequarters, that is to say, that now three persons live in urban to one who lives in a rural district. It is a well known axiom of hygienic science, that the health of a population, other things being equal, is in inverse ratio to its density. In plain Eng- lish, other things being equal, a population crowded together is more unhealthy than one that is scattered. The British people are becom- ing daily more crowded together, and hence, un- less some steps are taken, and that soon, tc counteract the effects of undue concentration. the national health must inevitably suffer. SOME REMEDIES. What are the steps which can be taken ? They are many and various, but we may mention a few.

Such matters are of the utmost importance to the working classes of Great Britain. They af- fect their daily lives, and their personal health, which, in the ease of many. i••• their only capital. Wrakiag to.• 11 ••• ' • • • • ,••1.1 therefore, hestir themselves to see that these vital questions .re carefully consid-red in their interests by ,lunicipal and Educational authorities. Let it he once known that the working classes are alive to the necessity of their children being trained m school in such a way as to develop their bodies, t ) render them capable of enjoying life, of be- .oniing useful citizens, and of producing a healthy generation to succeed them, and it will not be long before their elected servants, the Members of Municipalities, County Councils. and Educational Committees will find ways of offing a stop to the lamentable drain on the A.!;•ility of the nation which is daily taking place. It it be questioned whether it be a fact that the British people are deteriorating in physique. how comes it, as has often been stated in the public press without contradiction, that during the war in South Africa—out of 11,000 men who offered themselves from Manchester for the army. r;

===== 0002601_19050121_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
only 30(X) could oe acceptelt isa nertlns4,olll4 to physical defects, and that, of these 3,000, only 1,000 were found fit to be, sent abroad to fight the battles of their country F How comes it that the colonel of the Birmingham Volunteers has lately stated in public, that, on account of simi- lar bodily defects, he is obliged to reject annu- ally some 32 per cent, of the men who offer themselves? Let it, also, be remembered that these are volunteers, and presumably therefore men who have. been brought up in comparative- ly well-to-do homes in Birmingham. Moreover as these men would not, under ordinary circum- stances be required to serve in the field, we mak reasonably suppose that the physical tests to which they would be subjected in examination would not be as severe as those which the ordin- ary recruit of the regular army would be re- quired to undergo. Furthermore, from infor- mation which the writer of this article lately obtained, it appears that some 50 per cent, of the lads from certain British towns, who annually offer themselves for service in the British Navy, have to be rejected by the recruiters on the spot for similar reasons, and that the services of a further 25 per cent. are declined owing to the discovery of more obscure physical disabilities when the lads have reached headquarters.

OUR RECRUITS.

It cannot be denied that these facts point to b most serious condition of affairs, threatening the very foundations of British social, commer- cial, and national life.

But, indeed, statistics are not necessary. We only require to go into the more crowded parts a our large towns, and to use our eyes in order to perceive how weak, stunted, and ill-formed are many of The young men and young women who are to be foundin their midst.

" It is somewhat depressing." said the "Daily Graphic," in a recent issue, " to learn from good authority that, although the British recruit be- fore the late NM about equalled the German in height, his weight, the surer criterion of physi- cal strength and stamina, was considerably less." The above statement may, indeed, be charac- terised as " depressing," but if it be true, it is well that we should know it, in order that we may live in no fool's paradise, but may be able without loss of time to take effective steps to bring about a condition of affairs less hurtful to or pride, and more in keeping with the popu- lar, but at present, fallacious notion of the phy- sical superiority of the Briton. The Royal Commission for Scotland on Phy- sical Training has borne important witness to the unsatisfactory physical state of the Scotch people, and has suggested remedies which it is hoped that the Government and the local au- thorities will not be slow in adopting, but if the condition of the people of Scotland be so bad. we may be sure that that of the people of Eng- land and Wales, of whom over 12,000,000 are crowded into forty-four cities with an average population of over 270,000 each, must be still worse. • ROYAL COMMISSION REQUIRED.

As it is of the greatest importance that no- thing should be left to conjecture ki so import- ant a matter, the Government would be well ad- vised to follow the precedent they have set themselves in the case of Scotland, and appoint a Royal Commission to enquire into the physical ?ondition of the people of England and Wales. The terms of reference to the Scotch Commis- sion might well be adopted, mutatis snutendis, Is those under which the English Commission might be instructed to act, namely To enquire into the opportunities for physical training now available in the State aided schools and other educational institutions of Scotland. md to suggest means by which such training may be made to conduce to the welfare of the pupils, and further, how such opportunities may be increased by Continuation classes and other- wise, so as to develop, in their practical applica- tion to the requirements of life, the faculties of those who have left the day schools, and thus to contribute towards the sources of national strength. In the meantime the people of Great Britain, and especially the working classes living in our large cities, who compose that portion of our population which is the most directly interested in the subject, should bestir themselves, and use all the legitimate influence they possess to impress members of Parliament. Corporations, Town and County Councillors, Educational au- thorities and others, of the urgent necessity which exists to turn their most serious attention to matters appertaining to the health, strength, and physical condition of the people of this country, and to take instant steps to improve the physique of the Briti,,h race.

MEATH.

SECONDARY SCHOOLS IN CHESHIRE.

Important Conference.

THE RELIGIOUS TEACHING AT LYMM GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

The Cheshire County Education Committee held a meeting at Crewe, on Monday, Mr. C. E. Thornycroft presiding.—Dr. Hodgson, chairman of the Higher Education Committee, reported the result of a confer- ence held between that committee and deputation, from Crewe, Wallasey, Northwich, Nantwich, Knutsford Lymm, and other places, with regard to the provision of secondary schools to meet the requirements of those places. The deputation from Crewe suggested that a new secondary school should be built for 50 I boys and girls, on a site near the railway station, the present building being retained for evening technical classes and for certain day classes, but that the pupil teachers' centre should be held in connection with the new school. The deputation from Wallasey suggested that the County Council should take over the assets and liabilities of the present high school for girls. The cost of anew girls' school for 300 pupils was estimated at a sum of £12.000 to £lB,OOO, exclusive of site. Further accommodation for boys was required. The deputation agreed that a new school for 200 girls was needed. It was stated that there were 200 boys at the grammar school, and that with the increase in the population of the district provision should be made for 300 or 350 in the near future,either by rebuild- ing the_present school or by very extensive alterations to it. It was reported that Sir John Brunner, Bart., M.P., intended to build a new school at Northwich and a resolntion was passed suggesting to Sir John Brunner that it is desirable that the proposed new school should provide accommodation for boys boys as well as girls, and that the administrative sub- committee should be asked for their opinion as to whether the school should be a mixed or a dual school, and also as to the number of boys and girls for whom prcivision should be made. A deputation from Nantwich and Audlem met the committee. It was agreed that there was a necessity for a new mixed secondary school for about 250 boys and girls, and the governors of the present grammar school considered that the present site was suitable for the new school if adjoining property could be obtained. In this matter it was decided to re-consider the question after the receipt of a report from the Board of Education. A deputation from Knutsford also waited on the committee and laid their views before them. It was decided that the scheme for the linutsfoed Grammar School be arranged so as to provide for the school being a mixed school for boys and girls. A deputation from Lymm was received by the committee, who drew their attention to the Pact that the present scheme in regard to the management of the grammar school there provided that the head master should be a member of the Church of England, and that religious instruction shotild be given in accordance with the ' doctrines of the Church of England. It was decided to adjourn the conference so that the governors might consider the amendment 'of the school. Dr. Hodgson proposed the adoption of the report, and a discussion took place as to the desirability of extending the period for which loans were allowed for building secondary schools to sixty years, so as to reduce the annual charge for repayment. It was decided to urge the Local Governmeot Board to introduce a short Act of Parlia- ment dealing with the question.
