===== 0002601_19050121_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
Shortness of temper UNIONISTS AND is excusable,perhaps, SOCIAL REFORM. just now among the embarrassed friends of a Ministry which still shivers on the brink of a dissolution. While Mr. BALFOUR hesitates to take a step which must leact to defeat, his sup- porters in the Press cannot be expected to view very calmly the preparations of his opponents to take up the work he has so liberally mismanaged. Sir HENAY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN gave a sketch of this task in his speech at Stirling, dealing chiefly with its financial aspect. He gave some figures of the national expenditure, and urged the necessity of retrenchment. a is not an unreasonable contention; but the bare mention of retrench- ment is positively infuriating to the Unionist mind. Sir HENRY CAMPBELL.BANNERM&N has only to suggest that we might have an efficient Army for less than we spend on it, and the "Times" promptly accuses him of a desire to make his country utterly defenceleis in order to spend the money saved by this means on "visionary Socialistic schemes." Indeed the " Times" regards ! any proposal to reduce expenditure as tantamount to the abolition of the Army and Navy. Sir ! HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN is told that "his argument is just as good for aboli- ,tion as for retrenchment." So the people who read the article in the " Times " on Sir HENRY'S speech, but not the speech itself, may have the idea that the Liberal leader wants to abolish, or at any rate seriously to

weaken, our defences, although "we, and all Europe, are living on what may prove at any time to be the brink of a volcano." If there were even a scintilla of reason in this accusation, we ought to find SIR HENRY and his colleagues pro- testing against the considerable expense entailed by the equipment of the Army with new guns. But no such protest has been made. The new guns are absolutely indispensable, and the cost is net grudged. But the opposition have very justly omplained that men who talk so loudly about their solicitude for the safety of the Empire, and accuse their opponents of lack of patriotism,are the very men who neglected the artillery for years,and who have thrown our whole military organisation into lamentable confusion.

Everybody knows that a new Government .will have to take up this question of the Army, and strive to bring order out of the chaos created by Mr. BALFOUR. But the " Times " thinks fit to define the attitude of the Liberal party in this fashion: "It must not be supposed that Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN has no policy. He has one which, at least, has the merit of simplicity. He does not trouble himself about the security of the country or the reform of the Services. All that is necessary in his opinion is to cut down the expenditure upon them; and he intimated pretty distinctly that the first duty of a Liberal Government, on coining into power, will be to make sweeping reductions in the naval and mili- tary estimates." The simple answer to this charge is that a policy of reducing expenditure without taking any account of the security of the country and the reform of the Services is impossible. The little mistake of the " Times " is to assume that you cannot have security without the blind extravagance of a Government whi2h does not know its own mind. To go on heaping up the Army Estimates without taking the trouble to decide once for all what the British 1 Army ought to be, and what should reasonably be expected from it, is the policy we have witnessed 1 for the last ten years. The" Times " knows per- fectly well that the policy is totally wrong, but falls into a passion at the bare suggestion that we should practice a little economy. Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN said there were social prob- lems demanding attention, and that we should readjust our finances so as to deal with those prob- lems. There is nothing unusual in that position ; it belongs, indeed, to the v.)ry essence of wise administration and national welfare. But the " Times " sees in it the spectre of Socialism. "Everybody is to be encouraged to invent new wants by way of rising in the social scale." The housing of the poor is admittedly very bad; but 1 to hold out any prospect of an effort to remedy it is to encourage the poor to invent new wants. Let us not spread discontent among the tenants of insanitary dwellings. If this is to be the new Unionist battle-cry against the policy of social reform, the Liberal party will have no reason to feel uneasy.

" What will Mr. Balfour do ?" is a question agitating the minds of members of all political parties concerned in the government of the country. Parliament is due to re-assemble in less than a fortnight and the Premier will very soon be compelled to make up his mind whether it is worth while hanging on to office and dodging defeat throughout another wearisome session. Possibly Mr. Balfour has no "philosophic doubt" on the subject at all. He is said to be determined to hold on to the last, and one of " F.C.G.'S." inimitable cartoons pictlres his delight over a remark in the "Pall- Mall Gazette" that ministers need not give up until "their own consciousness of public useful- ness is exhausted." This is an inner consciousness and is evidently not easily exhausted. But the want of confidence in the House, and the clamour in the country may, and ought, to precipitate matters. Mr. Asquith said on Thursday evening that' according to all the canons ;of .lonstitutional propriety, aye, and according to all the rules of common sense the dissolution was not only due,but overdue. We had a Government—at least we had a body of men—who had shed one after another almost all their most distinguished and experienced colleagues, and were at this moment the weakest and the least respected Ministry that had held , office in this country for one hundred years." There probably never was a Govern- ment of which the country was so heartily tired as of this one of repeated " mess, muddle, and make believe," and the sooner an opportunity is given to the Progressive party to put things to rights and the better.

--- The Metropolitan Radical Federation, in antici- pation of a strong majority, asks what the new Government is going to do in the way of domestic legislation." Will it prove a giant with a weak brain . . . . or will it take the nation's affairs with a strong hand directed by a clearness of vision based on a firm belief in democracy, and filled with a determination to maintain peace, the retrench- ment of the Empire's wasted finances, and reform the many evils that now disgrace the fair name of England ? " We have every confidence in the strength, capacity, and wisdom of the men who are likely to control the destinies of the nation under a Liberal mandate. There need be no fears for a party possessing politicians of the mental—aye and moral—calibre of Sir H. Campbell Bannerman, Lord Rosebery, Mr. John Morley, Mr. Asquith, Mr. Lloyd George, Lord Stanley, Earl Spencer, Mr. Winston Churchill, Major Seely, Mr. Ivor Guest, and a host of others whose names recur readily to the memory. ' A brief suggested programme includes the aboli- tion of the breakfast table duties, the provision of old-age pensions, the taxation of land values, and universal suffrage. Were the next Government only to deal effectively with two of these measures it would have accomplished sufficient for posterity to ever hold it in honoured remembrance. The co-ordination of education in the borough has undoabtedly been the most important work to which the members of the Education Committee have applied themselves during the short period in which they have controlled educational affairs. It is only necessary to read the reports presented at the prize distribution in the Parr Hall on Thursday evening to find that a mvitsure of success has already been attained in the carrying out of a scheme fraught with great possibilities to the rising generation. Facilities now exist, it is pointed out, for every boy and girl in the town to be educated to a higher point of efficiency than hitherto. Commencing their education in the elementary school, students may when they reach the requisite level of learning enter the secondary school where the instruction is of a much more advanced order. The fact, however, has not been overlooked that the majority of chil- dren have to commence work when they are thir- teen or fourteen years of age, and for them there is provision at the eveninv schools and, at a later stage,7the Technical Institute and School of Art. It is distinctly gratifying to know that the new arrangements are working smoothly, that the improved facilities have been welcomed both by scholars and parents, and that the examination results denote a spirited co-operation on the part of the students. We hope the committee will attain their desire to strengthen the connection between the elementary and evening schools to ensure an immediate continuation of educa.ion, and that so honoured an institution as the Grammar School will soon occupy its just position as the highest rung in the local educational ladder

In securing the services of such an eminent and ardent educationist as Vice-Chancellor Hopkiu-

ri:. ..

===== 0002601_19050121_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
eautatton.

son to distribute the awards, the committee were indeed fortunate. His address, which we publish in full in another column, is well worthy of thoughtful perusal and it should have an encouraging effect upon pupils, staff and com- mittee alike. We were particularly impressed by the Vice-Chancellor's earnest advocacy of the best efforts being put forth for the enlightenment of the great mass of the people to whom a university career is impossible. The Vice-Chancellor is not one who closes his eyes to the need for attention to the practical side of education, and technical ' classes and the like should, in his view, equip the youth of the country for their special callings in life. Beyond that, however, true education sholld have a broadening and mellowing influence upon the mind—widening the horizion of view, as it was aptly put. We hope the Education Com- mittee will take to heart the Vice-Chancellor's emphatic declaration that the desiie for efficiency ought not to destroy regard for economy. "I believe there is a risk at the present time "he observed "of some reaction, owing to the ad- ditional burdens which have been rapidly placed upon the people." No one will, we imagine, dis • pute the necessity for sacrifice in the interests of future generations. It behoves all education authorities not to impose upon the good will of the ratepayers but to obtain the maximum output from existing equipment.

In a recent address on "Some Opportunities for Social Reforms," Sir Oliver Lodge maie thoughtful allusion to the problem of the unem- ployed and the dastitute, and its solution. Said he : "Relief to the poor should be given before complete destitution, and not after. If a man is capable of working hard, and also'of organising his life well, he is, ipso facto, above the average, and he will go to the top, or at any rate, fairly up ; he is not one of the failures. But the Poor- Law should legislate, he would not say for failures, but for those below the average in character and ability. And what they lack and he believed in many cases almost al of what they lacked, was organising power. Therefore, let them not be herded in cities,but be planted on the land, and made ae far as possible self-supporting. Had it ever struck them how odd 'it was that people ever died of starvation ? It was so little that people wanted to keep there alive; a man made very small demands upon existence. He did not know how little food a person could do with, but he knew that it seemed quite absurd, in a world of this productiveness, that anybody should be so grindingly poor as they often were. It was a disgrace ;. it was a thing which should be thoroughly attacked." A contribution to the question from such an eminent man as Sir Oliver Lodge is of more than ordinary valti3 and merits the deepest considera- by all social reformers and administrators of the Poor Law. Housing and helping the destitute certainly does not seem of much practical use or permanent value, while it remains a severe and unwelcome tax on the nation. There is something, we think, in Sir Oliver's idea that the able-bodied of such people might be "farmed out " as it were, and taught to organise their energies aid be self- reliant. The Workhouse regime does not rescue them from the perils of environment, and they still remain the drones of Society. And Society has less and less room for drones.

It;is not every undertaker who, given an order for a coffin, would linger in measuring the corpse, doubting whether he ought to question death. Most men engaged for such a duty are more perfunctory. The thought which comes home is how slender is the hope of anyone in a state of physical exhaustion raising an alarm if a mistake, has been made. The struggle for life is very painful, and very tenacious in many cases; but, as all nurses know, there generally comes a stage of simple weakness and approach to passivity—when. the dying seam to go, as it were, with the ebb tide. Persons in that state cannot cry or shout, and when they have been " laid oat "" even the nearest rAlatives often hesitate to stay in the room. There was a great, kindly wisdom in the religious custom of keeping watch over a loved body with lighted tapers all around But it is not practicable, except for a favoured few, in our day. Neither is it right that a corpse should be kept many days— for the sure test of decomposition—in the crowded rooms of the poor. Cremation would solve this and many other troubles; but until a Bishop or two has ordered - that his remains shall be so disposed of, it will not become general. That fantastic plecaution which obtains in some parts of France is also for the wealthy, and not pleasant to think of for them. It is a machine which !gives communication from the coffin to the ground. No, the best care is that adopted in Paris—for a medical [A ttendan t' s report in all cases to be verified by a Government official.

Within the pages of a little huff pamphlet there has been collected it set of stories that would separately make the fortune of a novelist of the Sue order. They are the authentic tales of people who have either peen buried alive, or at least have been placed in the final narrow shells unde the belief that they were dead. A man named Nicholas Lehow is now living in Australia, who 80Mb twenty-two years ago was fortunate enough to have strength to turn just as the undertaker's assistant was about to screw down the coffin. There is another ease, drawn from the "Boston Evening Transcript," of a Methodist minister, the Rev. E. R. Johnson, of Mulberry, Indiana, who finally passed away two years ago. He had the distinction of having died twice according t o the certificate of noted physicians. In the first instance, in 1869, the first part of the burial service had been read over the open coffin, when a gust of wind caused the door to bang, and the presumed dead man sat up in his coffin. Two weeks later,having recovered entirely from his cata-, • leptic condition, he preached in his old pulpit. These are instances out of examples of a score or more which the Society for the Pieventiou of Pre- mature Burial can cite, They were the lucky cases of discovery in time. There is not lipuch chance after the screw has been turned, There was one body ordered to be exhumed on account of suspected poisoning, and people saw what a struggle can take place—desperate, hopeless, and succourless. It had actually occured.

A ST. HELENS MANSLAUGHTER CASE.

Police Proceedings.

On Thursday at the St. Helens Police Court, before Messrs. B. B. Glover and W.. 1. Cook, an elderly man named Terence Dolan was brought up on the charge of causing the death of an old woman named Lucy Smith, on Saturday night. The Chief Constable stated that deceased was the housekeeper to a tinsmith named William Howitt, who resided at Higher Parr-street. Dolan, with his wife and boy, had lodged with Howitt for some time. About ten o'clock on Saturday night Dolan went to the house, and went upstairs. He called for a light, and a candle was brought upstairs to him by the woman Lucy Smith, who was 68 years of age. When she reached the second step from the top prisoner took the candle from her, and then gave her a push, which threw her to the bottom of the stairs. When she was picked up she was unconscious, and remained in that condition till ten o'clock on Sunday morning,when she died, the cause of death being fracture of the skull Evidence was called to bear out this statement. Dr. O'Keeffe said the cause of death was fracture of the skull.

Prisoner alleged that the old woman was knocked down the stairs during a scuffle with Howitt. He was committed for trial at the Liverpool Assizes.

BURIED IN A Alm. Mr. Brighouse held an inquest at Haydock, on Thursday, on the body of John Briscoe, forty-four, a colliery contractor, of Haydock, who was killed on Tuesday morning in the Leigh Pit. Haydock. Daniel Briscoe, deceased's half-brother, said he worked with him. On Tuesday they started work at 6 a.m. on a tunnel they were driving between the mine and four-feet mines. The tunnel was Ilft, wide and 6ft. high. All went well until half-past ten, whena sudden fall of roof took place, burying deceased. Witness was about five yards away, and immediately got help, and Briscoe was got out, but was found to be dead. The place was well timbered. Mr. Hall, the Government inspector, said he had come to tiro conclusion that if he bad visited the place before the accidenr he would hove passed it as safe. The Nry returned a verdict of " Accidental death "

A largo crowd was attracted to Market Gate last evening when it became known that a fire had broken out in the shop property in course of demolition there. It appears that during the day workmen had been engaged in burning refuse on the premises until recently occupied by Mr. Price, provision dealer. Apparently it remained smouldering and at 7.30 burst into flames which spread to the woodwork. The fire brigade turned out under Superintendent Turner, with the engine " Captain," and about an hour's work sufficed to extinguish the tire.

As for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS MINIMAL WATERS.—Advt. Snowstorm Disorganizes the Tram Service. A bitterly cold and windy day ended in a heavy snowstorm at Warrington on Monday. Vehicular traffic was rendered most unpleasant, and the tram- way service was greatly disorganized. The current failed frequentlyoowing to the snow getting between the wheels and the rails, and so breaking the circuit. One car accomplished a ten minutes journey in 21 hours, while on the Longford route the cars could not get beyond the Horse and Jockey Inn. The work of clearing the main thoroughfares was undertaken early on Thesday morning, extra hands having been employed by the Borough Surveyor and the streets were very rapidly made serviceable.

The Late Mr. Henry Lee. The committee of the Liberation Society passed a resolution on Monday respecting the death of Mr. Henry Lee, J.P., of Manchester. The committee placed on record their deep sense of the value of his services to the society's cause, not only as a member of their own body for a lengthened period, but also in connection with the North-West District Branch of the Society, and referred to his fidelity in the dis- charge of his religious, political and civic duties, his steadfast adherance to his cherished convictions, and his uniform geniality and courtesy in the conduct of public affairs.

Repairing Lock Gates. On Monday the work of repairing the side gates in the small locks at Latchford which were damaged some months ago, was completed. A gate weighing about 150 tons, was launched at Thelwall. A slip- way had been prepared there, and it was covered with soft soap and tallow, to enable the gate to travel down quickly. It was held in position by a strong Manilla rope, and at the word " go" the rope was cut and the gate slid down the slipway at a terrific rate and plunged into the water, in a similar manner to launch- ing a ship, except that the gate went to the bottom of the canal. It was then raised by a large crane until it was in a vertical position and afterwards carried to the locks, attached to two powerful tug boats. On arrival at the locks, it was lowered, and a diver was sent down to arrange the rollers, after which the gate was put into its proper position. A large number of men were engaged, and were under the supervision of Mr. W. Fox, the mechanical 'engineer for the Manchester Ship Canal Company, who carried out the work in an expeditious manner. FOR GLASS AND CHINA Of every description try • J. S. GARLAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55' Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. The Parnell of Wales.

Mr. David Lloyd-George was 42 on Tuesday. He was born in Manchester in 1863, the son of a Carnarvon school teacher, and was educated in a church school near Criccieth. Anglicans point out how ungrateful the Parnell of Wales—as he is sometimes termed—is now in turning upon the good mother church who thus guided his childish steps in the pursuit of know- ledge. He became a solicitor with his brother, and they built up a large practice in Carnarvonshire, and he got into Parliament at a bye-election in 1890. In politics a fighting man is welcome, and Mr. Lloyd- George soon showed his courage. He attacked the appointment of county court judges in Wales who were unable to speak Welsh, and this championship of Welsh nationalism won him much respect and popularity. Since then he has, it is generally admit- ted on both sides of the House, gone on increasing his reputation, and he is certain to be included in the next Liberal Cabinet. He did supremely well in denouncing the Beer war when the war was popular, and he achieved another great success in his brilliant attacks on the Education Bill. He has the Welsh readiness of speech and nimbleness of mind, and he can be very humorous when he chooses. He is an excellent debater, and can " draw " Mr. Chamberlain more skilfully than any other member in the House. He owes nothing to influence, but everything to his awn ability, pluck, and resourcefulness.—Oar readers will, of course, remember that Hr. Lloyd George is to be the principal sreaker at a Liberal meeting in Parr Hall on Friday, February 3rd.

FIRE GRATES of every deseription.—J. S. GAR LAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, & 55, Bridge- street, Warrington.—Advt. " Cinderella " at Higher Walton. A pretty operetta entitled " Cinderella " has been presented on Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday and Friday evenings of this week in the Parish 'Boom, Higher Walton. TENANT SUES LANDLORD FOR

MAKING A NOISE.

In the Chancery Court of Lancashire before Sir W. Hall, Vice Chancellor, sitting at the Chancery Court, St. George's Hall, Liverpool—Robertson v. Sugg. The trial of this action was set down for hearing on Wednesday, January 18th and was on the part of Alexander Robertson, plaintiff, who is a tenant of the defendant te restrain him from carrying on the business of a manufacturer of cricket bats, tennis racquets, hockey sticks also other athletic goods on the premises situated over and about his office at 12 Lord-street, in such a manner as to cause him annoyance and render it impossible for him to conduct his business. Plaintiff claimed injunction, costs and damages. Mr. Wilson instructed by Messrs. Simpson Munroe appeared for plaintiff and Mr. J. Rutherford instructed by Messrs. reebay and Lynch for defendant. Counsel for plaintiff in opening the case explained that he had inspected the premises and admitted that all rough work was carried out in the basement. That the finishing processes were completed in rooms imme- mediately over plaintiff's office and the noise caused by the workmen was such as to constitute a nuisance at Common Law.

--Tie—ViTe-dhancellor interrupted Counsel and sug- gested that as he remarked at the Motion for an interim injnnction, this was distinctly a case which ought to be compromised. That plaintiff was only paying a small rent and he should have arranged to seek another office rather than fight a long and expensive Chancery action. Counsel stated that plaintiff had been offered £llO already and that he had refused it. The Vice Chancsllor thought that was an unreasonable sum and defendants stated that they were prepared to repeat the offer made when the proceedings were com- menced to give Plaintiff £25 as compensation for dis- turbance of tenancy and Plaintiff to pay his own costs. This'offer was not accepted and the case proceeded again. The Vice Chancellor intervened and Plaintiff offered to accept £5O and costs but after considerable negotiations agreed to accept defendant's original offer of £25 for compensation each side to pay costs, the Plaintiff to give up his lease and find other premises as early as possible. Mr. Wilson pointed out that Plaintiff had incurred very heavy costs in bringing this action but Vice Chanceror Hall told Counsel that the question of costs did not in any way concern him. He simply tried the merits of the case and thought Plaintiff had been ill advised in bringing his action, His Honour in rising congratulated Counsel on settling the matter and as this was the last case the court then rose.

THE TRAM FARE.

Parish Council Take Action.

A special meeting of the Stockton Heath Parish Council was held In the ;National Schools on Thursday niaht. Mr. J. H. Clarke occupied the chair, and there were present, Messrs. Pearson, Whitley, Bouchier, Fairhurst, H. J. Westbrook, Warburton, Pilling and Hallows.

----.-. At the outset the Chairman read the following letter from the Town Clerk of Warrington :—ln reply to your letter of the 30th ult. I have to inform you that the Town Council have confirmed the recommendation of the Tramways Committee that the fare between ftylands-street and Victoria-square shall be lid. With regard to the resolution contained in your letter I have to remind you that the offer to fix the "fares within the parish as far as practicable at the same mileage rate as in the borough" was contained in my letter to you of September 8, 1900. That, with other , proposals which the Corporation offered in that letter, was made with the object of inducing the Parish Coun- cil to support the Corporation in their application for the order. The terms submitted by the Corporation were not accepted, and the Parish Council thought fit to oppose the application for the order, and relied upon , the Cheshire County Council and the Board of Trade to , get their demands conceded. All the conditions the Board of Trade thought fit to grant to theCountyCoun- , cii are contained in the Order itself and the Parish , Council must now rely upon these conditions. The Chairman, dealing with the letter, said the Coun- cil had been assured before the order was obtained that there would be no greater charge in the fares up to : 1 Victoria Square than a penny, provided that the tram- ways were paving. The Parish Council asked for the 1 roads to be widened and they offered to widen them. If , they (the Parish Council) disagreed in some minor things those were the fund unental conditions. He (the chairman) accepted the Town Clerk's word. There were several members of the Parish Council who dis- agreed at that time, and said that whatever was done it should he put in writing. (Applause.) Mr. H. J. Westbrook said it was to be regretted that the question as to the "assurance" which had been referred to involved a personal matter with the Town Clerk, who he had always found ready to listen to the views of the other side on this question and to meet them so far as consistently could be done.having regard to his position. At the same time he thought the Town Clerk had forgotten the circumstances, and was some- what confused in his collection of what had really taken place. Personally he did not doubt but that there had been a misunderstanding and they had only themselves to blame for implicity relying on anyone's word and not insisting on the matter being put down in black and white. Many people believed that the matter of the penny fare would settle itself, that was to say that the Stockton Heath people could se avoid the use of the trams as not only to escape the additional halfpenny fare but also to decrease the revenue on the penny stage. This was not a pleasant position to take up and the question was whether the Council itself could do anything with regard to what they contended was a distinct breach of faith on the part of Warrington The provision in the order for an application to the Board of Trade for a revision of the fares did not operate until the expiration of three years from the opening of the railway and by that time the three-half- penny fare would of course be a matter of history. He feared that they ass Council could not usefully do any- thing but the individual public could do everything. There was a matter however which the imposition of the three-halfpenny fare had brought into prominence, and that was the question of cheap fares for the labouring classes. Mr. Westbrook quoted the clause in the order referring to the matter, and said that the minimum fare for the labouring artisan class was fixed at a halfpenny a mile or a frac- tion thereof. The distance between Rylands-street and Victoria-square was 11 furlongs so that the fare for the class mentioned would be one penny all the way. (Hear, hear.) Not a word had been said, judging from the newspaper reports, in the Borough Council about the ' cheap fares, and he thought it was the duty of the Parish Council on bekalf of the class of ratepayers referred to, to see that the Corporation fulfilled the obligations placedupon them by the order. (Hear, hear.). Mr. Bouchier and Mr. Hallows followed in support of the remarks of Mr. Westbrook and it was resolved that the clerk should write to the Town Clerk of Warrington and enquire what, if any provision had been or was being made to comply with the terms of the Order with reference to cheap fares for the labouring classes.

Death of a Well Known Farmer. Hany of our readers will regret to learn of the death of Mr. Stephen Dean, Poplars' Farm, Orford, who passed away on Thursday morning at the age of seventy. The deceased gentleman was one of the best known farmers in the district. For many years he carried on the business of a farrier, but when the Veterinary Surgeons Act came into operation he failed to avail himself of its provilons within the prescribed time. He had, however, continued to render valuable assistance to farmers, and was held in high esteem. He was a regular attender at the Warrington Market and a familiar figure at the Corn Exchange. A member of the Wesleyan denomination, he worshipped at Padpte Chapel, and was a member of the Society. His continual exposure to varied weather conditions in the discharge of his work resulted in an attack of bronchitis, which proved fatal. He leaves a widow ) and three sons.

LOCAL EDUCATION.

PRIZES AND CERTIFICATES

DISTRIBUTED AT THE PARR HALL.

Speech by Vice-Chancellor Hopkinson.

THE FUTURE OF THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL

The Parr Hall was the scene of an interesting and largely-attended gathering on Thursday evening when I Vice-Chancellor Hopkinson, K.C., M.A., L.L.D., Victoria University, Manchester, distributed the prizes and certificates gained by the students at the School of Aft, Secondary School and Pupil Teachers' classes, and the Technical Institute. It is evident that these important branches of the Education Com- mittee's work are making gratifying progress, and the meeting had been looked forward to with considerable pleasure. Prior to the speech-making an enjoyable organ recital was given by Mr. W. H. Payton, Mae. Bac. and was much appreciated. The proceedings were presided over by Mr. F. Monks, J.P. (chairman of the Education Com- mittee) who was supported on the platform by Vice- Chancellor Hopkinson, Mr. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., the Revs. Morley Stevenson, N. Wilson, and H. A. Lester,Aldermen Bennett and Smethurst, Councillors I Fulton Smith, Ellison, and Craik, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, IJ. D. Buckton, J. Upson, H. Kingdon, G. Walker, E. Plinston, T. May, J. Moore Murray (secretary and director), E. S. A. Robson (principal of the Secondary School), and members of the teaching staffs. The 1 successful students occupied seats on the platform gallery. 1 THE FUTURE OF THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL. I The CIIUMAN said he had given several addresses lon the question of education lately, and he did not intend to weary his hearers that night, but he wished to give what was practically an announcement. The Education Committee had no intention of interfering with any existing educational institution in this town. He referred to the Grammar School. The object of the Committee was to co-ordinats education, so that every youth who had got the will, the energy and the brain power should have the' opportunity of being thoroughly well educated from the bottom to the top, and then leave him or her to make the best use of the knowledge they had acquired in their future course of life. That was the object of the Education Committee. They had no wish to interfere whatever with any existing institution, and they hoped that the Governors of the Grammar School would soon put themselves in line with, what he might call, the stream of education in Warrington so as to bring the Grammar School into the posidon which it ought to hold, and which he hoped it would hold very shortly. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19050121_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
TEIE SCHOOL OF ART.

The Director then road the annual report on the I School of Art as follows :—The work of this school during the session under review compares very favour-- ably with the previous years. The number of students in the day classes increased by 136, whilst the number of evening students was less by 42. The total number of students was 250, or an increase on all classes of 1 students of 19. The change in the number of day and evening students was due to the re-organization of the training of pupil teachers. At the beginning of the session pupil teachers began to attend at fixed times in the day instead of in the evening as hitherto, and the classes were arranged to meet their requirements. The results of this re-oreanization are to be seen in the increase in the percentage of successes at the examinations of the Board of Education in those subjects taken by pupil teachers. In blackboard draw- ing the percentage increased from 61 to 87, and in free- hand from 27 to 83. If the work of the school is judged by the result of the Art examinations, great progress has been made, the percentage of successes having increased from 45 to 65. By a consideration of the results of the individual sub- jects it will be found that the students have concen- trated their efforts on fewer subjects and apparently made greater progress. This is further borne out by the " Honours ' list. Last year there were no "Honours," but this year the works of Paul W. Brunt, Lilian M. Carnson, Harry Sherburn, and Edith L. Smith, submitted for the art class teacher's certificate, were accepted by the Board of Education. Miss Lilian N. Carnson was awarded a prize for her work, and, in addi- tion to these, Mr. Thomas B. Whitby was awarded a free studentship by the Board of Education. la the sum er of 1903 the assistant art mistress resigned, and Ifr. George S. Pendlebury was appointed assistant art master. In addition to the school of art proper, the staff have been responsible for the art instruction to the scholars in the secondary school. THE SECONDARY SCHOOL.

The report on the work of this school was submitted as follows :—At the beginning of the Session the whole system of the training of pupil teachers had been revised in such a manner that, unless immedilte steps were taken, the supply of local teachers would cease. It would consequently be necessary to compete with other authorities in order to find teachers for the 'elementary schools, and, at the same time, shut out the boys and girls of Warrington from the te tching profession unless they went out of the town for their instruction. The committee, to meet this clifficalty. established the Secondary school in the Technical Institute which was then only being used for the pupil teachers and the cookery centre. ?tr. A. Gow, 8.A., B.Sc., was appointed head master with an efficient staff, most of whom were trained teachers and graduates of some University. An entrance examination w‘s held in September, 1903, and 128 students were enrolled in the school, together with 18 preparatory pupil teachers and 91 pupil teachers. The establishment of the school has been fully justified as there are now more applicants for admission than can be accommodated. Little can be said of the school at present. That there are the essentials of success has recently been shown by the spirit displayed by the staff and scholars in organising successfully a bazaar, the result of which will enable the scholars to fit out their recreation ground. The grants obtained from the Board of Education were exceptionally good for the first year, and the Board of Education has reported that " the school has made a very successful beginning.

THE TECHNICAL INSTITUTE.

The Director read the report for the technical in- stitute as follows :—As usual, ta year opened with a large number of changes in the staff, but this year they were exceptional ; Ir. A. Gow being appoinled head master of the Secondary school and 'Principal of the Technical Institute, whilst the new staff of the school were engaged in teaching in the Technical classes. This arrangement assured a treater interest in the successes and welfare of the students, as, naturally when a teacher's whole interests are centred in on& building, greater efficienoy must result than when their interests are dispersed over different schools and towns. Two important changes were made in the organisation of the school. Students ware admitted to the classes on condition that they satisfied the teachers that they were fully prepared to under- take the work, and the classes were arranged in groups or courses to most the necessities of the apprentices engaged in the more important trades in the Borough.! It is yet too early to judge of the emend of these arrangements but there can be no doubt that the quality 1 of the students was considerably improved, and their regular attendance MIA most marked. The examination results show an increased number of first classes in the 11 advanced stages, whilst three students gained "honours" 1 in inorganic chemistry, four students gained "honours" 1 Ira plumbing, one student gained the teachers' certificate for woodwork, one student gained the sixth place in the Lancashire and Cheshire examination in arithmetic, and one student gained a distinction in cookery. The per- centage of successes was greater in each of the groups of Science, Technology, and Commerce. During the year the evening continuation schools have been brought into closer touch with the technical classes. The oommittee feel that as a remit of the year's work they have made some progress towards the co-ordination of education in the Borough. Facilities now exist for every boy and girl in the town to be educated to a hid:et-point than hitherto. Boys and girls may pass from the elementary schools to the secondary school, or if circumstances will not allow them to stay at school after they are 13 or 14 years of age -:.hey nen pass into the evening schools and then on to the Technical Institute and School of Art. The scheme, however, is incomplete as the connection between the elementary schools and the evening schools requiries strengthening and the Committee are not without hopes that they will he able to devise a method by which boys and girl leaving the elementary schools will immediately continue their education at the Even- ing schools, and so be well prepared to enter the Technical School to obtai n'a the retir ii riwlslge of their daily work. Nothing can be said at present about the Grammar school but the Conimittee hopes that it will fall into the general scheme and occupy that posi- tion which is its by right of long continued and useful service to the town.

VICE-CHANCELLOR HOPKINSON'S ADDRESS. THE NEED FOR CULTURE AMONG ALL CLASSES. PRACTICAL TEACHING ENCOURAGED. • Vice-Chancellor HOPKINSON, who was very cordially received, said he would like first to express his thanks to those members of the staff who had enabled them with such celerity and comfort to dispose of the pleasantest part of the evening, that on his part of handing the prizes, and on the part of the successful scholars of receiving them. (Applause.) Then ho would like to express his hearty congratulations, as he had not been able to do so individually, to those who had been successful in gaining certificates and prizes, and to express the wish that what they had received might be only the beginning landmark in their course—a course which might lead to many future successes, and what was much more important, to the beginning of doing good work in the world. (Applause.) Perhaps most of all—and that appealed to one personally, he would like to express specially his congratulations to the parents whose children had been succcessful, because if it was pleasant to win successes oneself, it was distinctly more pleasant to feel that the next generation for which they were responsible was winning successes, and he was sure he could give the parents his most sympathetic and hearty congratulations. (Applause.) With regard to the unsuccessful students, his vmptthies lay a great deal in that quarter. He thought it was Lowell, the American writer, whose works he had had the pleasure of handing to one or two students, whe said that whatever successes in life they might achieve, they would probably find that there was somebody else who had not won any re- ward, who had not gained any fame, but who richly deserved success even more than the successful ones themselves. (Applause.) He did not mean to say any-

thing so absurd as that those who were unsuccessful were usually better than the successful—that would be a falsehood. But he did say that there were some unsucoessful who richly deserved success. From his own experience, which perhaps they might think was becoming very long indeed, he could say that they would find in after-life that if they were working for the sake of the work itself, they would probably, whether they succeeded or failed, I not be entirely disappointed. If, on the other hand, their eyes were mainly on the reward, it was long odds that they would be disappointed. Some men and women would succeed at least as much as they deserved, I possibly not as much or more than they expected, but as a rule they found that probably the hardest and best work they did would not receive very much recognition. Was it not a good thing to have somewhere about them apiece of work which they looked back to as having accomplished, and which they joyed in the fact of having accomplished, and for which they had never had any I recognition of any kind whatever? (Applause). That I was the kind of thing of which people ought to be I proud. On the other hand, he could say for his part that little bits of easy work he had done had sometimes recelved so much kindly recognition that he had almost been ashamed of himself, because there had not been something more worthy of the praise which that: kind of work had gained. Now he was in a little difficulty, for he had been saying that day that he thought the time had come when speeches on education ought.to cease, and above all, when conferences on education ought to cease— (laughter)—and that when anybody made a speech on education in a promiscuous and general way—and he would offer the suggestion to their member of Parlia- ment whom he was glad to see present—he should be liable to fourteen days without the option of a fine if the speech exceeded ten minutes duration. (Loud laughter.) Anyone who was guilty of being an accessory to the fact, and was even more guilty than the one who had been seduced into making a speech should have a calendar month, and with regard to those who promoted conferences on education that ought to be a criminal conspiracy, and above all without the benefit of clergy. (Laughter and applause) To speak candidly, he was there because the Rev Morley Stevenson asked him, and his experience led him to think that those were not very well-conditioned people who refused a request of Mr. Stevenson's. (Applause.) Occupying as he did, the position of being the nominal head of a large University at their own doors, he felt it his d ity and pleasure to make an acquaintance with the work which was going on in whatshe might call his educational diocese—that was the district within rn oder ate range of the University— (Applause)—and, above all, to assure those who were working,—the teachers, the members of local education committees who gave so much unrernunerative labour to the public cause, the parents and scholars—that they were engaged in one great forward movement. Let it be known that they all had certain ideals which they were going to place before them, and that although they were not going to talk of those ideals every day when they ought to be engaged in practical work, they had something towards which they aimed, and they meant to march forward as hard-working teachers, dili- gent scholars.self-sacrificing parents,and also as business nen who would spend their time unstintedly in the work- ing out of business details. There was a great deal of truth in what one of his colleagues said : " What we want most of all in all our work is to do it 10 per cent better if we ean." If they all made up their minds to that endeavour they would make progress very soon. Here in Warrington they had established the work of their Education Committee. He believed it would be found that differences which divided men very much on questions of politics, religion, and many other subjects sank into insignificance comparatively for the time being. He did not mean to say that those questions were not of vital importance in themselves, but at all events, when they were engaged in common work with common objects they could see that those differences for the time being were irrelevant, and they could work together with a single eye to promote the public good in the particular direction in which the effor:s of such a committee were aimed. (Applause.) Two principles which were of the utmost importance had, he believed, been recognized in the work of the Warrington Com- mittee. The first was that they recognized the fact that although they might have made up their minds to promote the progress of education, so far as was con- sistent with that aim they meant to do it economically. He believed there was a risk at the present time of some , re-action, owing to the additional burdens which had been rapidly placed on the people. They must lea,rn that it was necessary to make sacrifices of time and money if the next generation was to be properly educated, but he did not think it was at all an unwise course which had induced them in Warrington to use their large and 1 splendid building to its utmost capacity, in the day- time for pupil teachers' centre and secondary school, and in the evening for technical instruction. Whether I that was a permanent arrangement or not he did not I know, but what he thought they ought to look at was to get from such buildings and equipment as they had the very utmost output, to work them to their fullest capacity, always remembering that an efficient staff was the first requirement of all educational progress. (A.polaus 3.) They might, he believed, have good educational progress in a school conducted in a shed behind a haystack, but they could not have that progress in the finest equipped building they could imagine, unless they had a thoroughly com- petent staff, and nnless the members of it were pre- vented from being constantly in a state of anxiety with regard to their own private concern. (Applause.) Alluding to the educational ladder the speller observed that it had been said that they wanted to open the door for the poorest from the eretnentary school to the University. There was a truth in that, but that truth was sometimes pushed too far. 119 trusted that among those to whom he had had the pleasure of deliver- ing prizes, there might be some who would , come to the Uaiversity in Nlincheater, who I would show by the SlileeSSOS they achieved there, and by the work they did that they were thankful I for the advantages which had been placed at their 'disposal in Warrington. We could not, however, shut our eyes to the fact that those were and must inevit- ably be a small minority. We could not fix our whole educational system with regard to those who would come to the Universities. (Hear, hear.) We must recognize the fact that a large number from the time of completing their elementary school education must go to the work of earning their daily bread. He hoped all engaged in educational work would remember that education ought not and must not cease with the public elementary school, for any class of thd population. (Applouse.) And here he was not referring to the educa- tion wuich the practical life of man, pr eperly lived, must inevitably give. We wanted our continuation schools and technical classes, and he wis not one of those who, for tno:nent. affected to despise those practical subjects of domestic economy which, he was glad to say, were taught in the Warrington Technical schools, and which, assuming them to be well taught, were amongst most useful subjects to which education could be directed. (Applause). Those who thought of nothing but litera- ture and 'science, and forgot the necessity of learning cookery and dressmaking had a very great deal to learn. "(Applause.) For his own part, he believed that not even the wildest national extravagance of the most lavish of Governments was liable for so much national waste, whether in the energy of the people or in actual hard cash. as the deficient cookery. which existed among helthought, he might say, the majority of the people of this country. (Applause.) He thought we ought to have in all evening ctasses a special training which would fit men and women for their own special work in life. He did not mean to say they could replace the work or the workshop, the counting house, or the home by what could be t mght in evening classes. What they could do was to make the mind more ready to receive new impressions under new conditions, and to make the workman or anyone else more adaptable to new conditions under which they might be placed, by teaching the principles of which practical work was best. They might extend the usefulness of the classes by teaching not merely the subjects which related to industry and the work which people did primarily to earn their daily bread, but by doing all they could to awaken an interest in science, literature and history among all classes of the community. We were now a people demo erotically governed. He believed it was of vital import- ance that we should cultivate amongst all classes of our people from the earliest age the kind of spirit which looked beyond our own immediate time and place and widened the horizon of view that which was sometimes called culture. That class of education was, he thought, open to all, and from nothing had he derived more encouragement since undertaking educational work in the North Of England, then the conference hold the other day in Manchester of representatives of trade and co-operative societies, when the representatives of those huge bodies of workingmen came and said: "What can your universi- ties do for us to satisfy this desire of ours ?—not a I n• • , estre to go up a ladder which will rnake us leave the class to which we belong, not to turn Us from being workingmen into being something else, nor, on the other hand, even to make us more efficient for the pur- pose of earning our daily bread in the workshop, but to give us that which will give us a broader outlook in life, that which will employ the whole of our mental energies and make us broader-minded, stronger, and better men." The extent of that demand was the most hopeful educational sign in the country at the present time. In regard to secondary education he was glad they in Warrington were going on the lines of recogniz- ing the work done by existing institutions by helping them within the new sphere of their work—supplement, ing rather than supplanting the work done by existing institutions. It was in regard to secondary schools that the English system of education had been most deficient. He hoped that they would soon have within the range of the pockets of a large portion of the parents in every large town, a thoroughly good school both for boys and girls at reasonably low fees, and to which a fewcould be taken with scholarships, and that the school would be filled by the children of parents who were willing to make a certain sacrifice in order to keep theirchildren longer at school. There he thought they would find that they would get rid of the great problem of English education in huge centres of population existing in various parts of the country— that of parents Naho could not afford to send their chil- dreriawn.v in order to'continue their education up to the age of 16, 17, or even 18 years. 'They would probably have a great deal of time and thought to devote to their work in the future, but they could look forward to being replaced before very long long by a generation better instructed than themselves.

B

What they wanted to learn perhaps most of all were the things they did not like. He believed that when they had learned to do that without grumbline, because the work was there to be dorm, they had made the best kind of progress, and they were stimulated by the work which became a pleasure. They should learn to do what was disagreeable, and byand by they would find that attacking difficulties almost became a habit of mind, and that was one of the things in moral education which he thought ought to be inculcated to the advan- tage of all scholars. The calling of the teacher was one which might be obscure and unknown but it was for the progress of the world, and it was the glorious task of trying to ensure that the next generation should be better instructed, more capable, and have higher ideals than that which had gone before. (Applause.) ALDERMAN BENNETT AND IDEALS. Alderman BENNETT pleiposed a vote of thanks to Vice- Chancellor Hopkinson for his instruction address that night: His very presence, said Alderman Bennett, was an inspiration and the address which they had listened to was one which they would not forget for a long time to come. They wished to take their peeper place in connection with the great neighbouring Universities of Manchester and Liverpool, their local Oxford and Cambridge if he might so term them, and they would not be satisfied until their local education system was more closely in touch with those institutions. They had listened that night to very stimulating advice from a man who could speak with authority. He was parti- cularly struck with the breadth which characterised the address. He sometimes thought from his short experi- ence in connection with education that they were apt to be overweighted with mere machinery, and he must confess that it had been surprising to find how much absolutely necessary detail there was. By this mass of detail there was a danger that they should overlook the larger ideals for which really education authorities came into existence. If they took to heart the words uttered so eloquently that night, they would never do that. Vice-Chancellor Hopkinson had held the standard high before them. It was pleasant to see their young friends come forward in such numbers to receive the beautiful books and many felt that education would lead to other prizes and possibly the attainmect of wealth. That,however,was not the real object. It was to set before them the highest object and the only I object worthy of striving for, that of doing good work in the world. He hoped in future they would set themselves an even higher ideal and do even better work than they had ever done before. (Applause.) MR. ROBSON AND SECONDARY EDUCATION. Mr. E. S. A. ROBSON, on behalf of the staff and pupils ; at the Technical Institute present that night, seconded the vote of thanks. In considering the question of secondary education, it seemed to him to form is pivot on which the whole scheme of education revolved. If he might borrow a simile from the football field, he might say it formed the half-back line. On the one hand, it stimulated the elementary school and on the other hand,it was a feeder to those higher educational institu- tions such as the Training College, the University and the Institution of Technology. Another point which had been considered as a very important factor in the success of secondary education was the idea of a con- cordat between the governors and the managers of Technical Institutions and the local employers of labour. (Hear, hear.) He thought on the one hand that Technical Institutions should turn out pupils who during their period of training should have constantly borne in mind the future commercial application of their knowledge, and, on the other hand, the employer of labour should take advantage of the technical institute and give every opportunity for progress and rapid advancement to the students. Such a scheme was in operati on in the United States and Germany and it was one which he thought might be tried with great success in the County Borough of Warrington. As to the Secondary school. of War- rington and the local Universities, he could hardly help thinking that they were like two rival lovers who were seeking the hand of a fair maiden, represented by the education authority of Warrington. (Laughter.) He could assure the Vice-Chancellor for the maiden ' who had been wooed that the impression he had given that night had been a most excellent one—(laughter and applause)—and when the time came and the delicate negotiations for some affili- ation scheme between their school and the Universities, that excellent impression would be fully borne in mind (Applause). He was glad to see the emphasis which the Vice Chancellor pat on the importance of manual train- ing, domestic work and woodwork. In connection with this, he would like to draw their attention to the remarks made last week by the Bishop of Manchester to the effect that such a scheme if carried out might offer possibly a solution for that thorny and difficult problem—the problem of the unemployed. (Applause). The motion was cordially carried and Vice Chanzellor Hopkinson briefly thanked the audience and wished every success to the educational institutions of the town in the future.‘ THE CHAIRMAN THANKED. The REV. MORLEY STEVENSON proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Monks for presiding. He said he did not think that anyone present could have witnessed the pro- ceedings that evening, without realising to some extent at all events the very multiple conditions under I which the Education Committee of Warrington had to work. They had heard that night, the reports of the S3hool of Art, the Secondary School, the pupil teachers' centre and the Technical School,and they might have heard, if the conditions had favoured it of elementary schools and the evening schools. All these branches of education came under the management of the Education Committee, and it meant a great amount *' of work, and to keep the reins of that Committee in his' hands required that tee chairman should be very skilful. 7he chairman had not only to be master of the details of the various education agencies of the town, but he had to drive the Education Committee itself, and of course the Education Committee was as multiple as the agencies it managed. Some represented education, some' represented the town, some represented the teachcrsi, some represented certain institutions, and ell represented the ratepayers. Mr. Stevenson proceeded to refer tie; the various schools, and (continuing) he said when they, thought of all those things, they would see that the ' gentleman to whom they entrusted the chairmanship of the committee had no easy task. Mr. Mooncs in reply said it was always easy to drive a willing team that was always ready for the collar, and he could say that as far as ha could judge, ecluene tionists were all of one spirit, and that was to do the best they could in the interests of education at the least cost consistent with efficiency. Whatever success they might have attained arose from the faot that they had a most devoted committee who did the wor in the very best possible way they could. He did not mean that they made no mistakes, but they made the fewest they possibly could. (Applause). The proceedings closed with the singing of the National Anthem.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

DRUNK IN COURT. James Harris and Mary Harris, 15, Pierpoint-street appeared before the Warrington magistrates on Monday to answer a charge of neglecting their five children.! They were both,it was stated,under the influence of drink, ! and the woman was declared by therhiefiConstable to be unfit to plead.—Mr. Browne prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., and said the case had been under supervision for about two years, and there had, in turn, been improvements and retrogression. Now, the defendants were regularly so helplessly drunk that it was utterly! impossible for the children to be allowed to stay with them any longer.—Mr. William Smith,superinten- dent of insurance, and who for some years ha, worked among the poor in the neighbourhood of All Saints' Mission, said he had had the defendants in hand for six years. They were both equally bad in their con- duct. On Boxing Day witness saw the man in a beastly state of drunkenness and took him home. On entering the house he found the wife lying drunk across the hearthrug, anol the wonder was that her hair had not caught fire, as it was very near the grate. The hou,3 was in a filthy state, and the children were left to the mercy of anyone.—Mrs. Slater, defendants' next door neighbour, said defendants were drunk three or four times a week. On one occasion, one of the children, who had been ill, was turned out with only one shoe and stocking on, while another was barefoot.—The woman became very noisy in Court, and the magistrates decided to remand both defendants in custody for a week. CBE 1P BEER—ME AN TRICK NE AR WA RRINGTON. I At the County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Patrick! Higgins, farm labourer, of Rixton, was charged with obtaining by means of a trick four glasses of beer value! fid, the property of Thomas Caldwell Rigby, landlord of the" Black Swan " Hotel, Rixton, on January 14th. He pleaded guilty.—The landlord stated that on the day in question prisoner came into the house in company, with three other companions. He supplied them with eight pints of beer fo: which the right money was paid. Four more glasses were ordered and under the impression that the coin tendered was a shilling, he placed it in the draw. it proved to be an advertising check. Another order WAS given and a piece of gilded metal advertising cigarettes, was pre- sented for money. He detected it, and gave informa- tion to the police. Witness suffered from short sighted- ness.—P.C. Smith stated that when he searched the prisoner he found a number of similiar cheques in his pockets.—Prisoner said he only paused them for a bit of fun.—The magistrates thought it was a very mean trick, and a fine of 10s. and costs was imposed. NEWSBOYS ROB A CHARITY Box. Before Mr. John Fairclough and other magistrates at the Warrington Borough Police Court on Thursday, Peter Collins, aged 9 and Thomas Collins aged 13,news- boys, residing at 4, Stoney-yard, off Bank-street were charged with having stolen a box containing 25., the property of the Poor Children's Society from the Central Hotel, Horsernarket-street,on Wednesday night. The facts of the case were to the effect that the boy Peter was seen by the licencee (Mr. Berry) warming his hands in front of the fire in the smoke-room. Shortly afterwards he disappeared, and Mr. Berry then missed the box. He went after the boy and caught him as he was leaving the premises with the box in his possession. The boy told him that his elder brother who was outside had arranged with him to steal the box.— Superintendent Monaghan said the boys had practically no home, and were neglected. The elder boy had been previously convicted of theft, and he was afraid if they were not separated he would lead the younger brother astray. The magistrates ordered Peter to receive six strokes with the birch, and to be detained in the cells until evening. The elder boy Thomas was sent to a reformatory school for five years.

He filled a cup with boding water for the purpose of shaving and placed it on the table He then went into the back kitchen, and during his absence, which was only for a few seconds, deceased' who had been sitting on the hearthrug, got up and Pulled the table cloth, causing the cup to tilt over.
