"STRATEGY TRIUMPHANT."

By Thomas Saunders.

AIMIOR OF:—" Sir ArchihAd's Opponent," "The MIA Woman of Dre9tone Re3tory,"

There were two things I was noted for in our little town of Kirby. They were —my unlimited auda-3ity, and a remarkable resemb' Ince I possessed to the Earl of Kirby, the representative of the aristocracy in our neighbourhood. Of course I could not be reaware of these two facts, for our town being only a small one, everything and everybody were known to each other. For several weeks before the Coronation of King Edward the seventh, that event was the subject of all the conversation. Every day I could hear my fellow- clerks discussing their plans for seeing the paaee it. I, too, would Faye spoken of my plans had fnot bean recently spending a lot of money on some glass houses I had put up on a bit of ground round about our house. It wanted just three days to Coronation morning. In our office the talk was going on 89 11911 V and I v beginning to be reconciled to the thought that I was the only one of eight clerks who would not see the show. Somehow or other it leaked out in the course of the morning, that the L-trl of Kirby had sprained his ankle the day before,hilst viewing with his agent some pardy-built villas on his estate. The fact seemed to suggest a whole host of thoughts to me: but I wisely kept them to myself. I knew that his Lordship would have already made his arrangements for viewing the historic function in the Abbey among the the Peers of the Realm. I knew, as well, that he would not only be agitated by the vain from his injured foot, but that he would be literally mad with vexation that it should have happened now. No wonder that the thoughts which I welcomed to my mind that afternoon should cause me to make several wrong calculations in the work I had in hand. That night I ate my evening meal without speaking a solitary word to my wife. She wondered ; but 11, aew that as T stared at the pattern of the paper on the wall opposite to me, 1 was planning out something in which, if I were successful, I should be able to crow over every clerk in our °face. After the meal was over I took ray hat and stick and strolled off in the direction of Kirby castle. I learned afterwards that my wife secretly sent my eldest boy to watch my doings,thinking heransiety, that a suicidal tendency was upon me. Arriving at the noble .ntranc 3 to his Lordship's park passed in, saluting, as I did so, the aged lodgekeeper who stood leisurely by. Passing up the great flight of steps whieh led to the entrance of the hall, I gave one strong pull at the bell and waited, impatiently and nervously screwing the ends of my moustache to very fine points. A moment only passed, and I was handing in my card to the attendant who stood before me, and asking for art interview with his Lordship. At first I was informed that it was impossible to see him,owing to his accident ; but after stating that my business was most important, word came down that I might be shown up to him. I was shown into a most comfortable room, where the Earl reclined in an easy chair, nursing the injured foot on a small stool in front of him Turning his head towards me as I entered,he enquired the business which was so important that it brought me even to the sick chamber. _ _ .

Now! knew that if I told him straightforwardly the busine,:; which had brought me, I should fail in my purpose. I relied on my resources, and began the pre- liminary yarn which was lead up to the all-importb.nt business.

You must know that the Earl and I were about the same age. When we were both about fourteen years old, I had been the means of saving his life. This is how it happened. One day I was passing along the lane which skirted the estate of his father, the old Earl, when suddenly I heard the noise of a horse's hoofs behind me. I don't know what impelled me to tutu round before the horse got up to me, but I did so, and saw that it was rider- less. I saw, as well, that as it galloped towards me it dragged something along with it. As it came nearer, I perceived tint it was its rider who was being dragged along by the stirrup. It was only the work of a minute for me to shout and put up my arms in front of it. This made the horse rear up, and as it did so, I jumped at its bridle and secured it. I found the rider to be the present Earl, then a lad of fourteen like myself. He was badly knocked about, but undoubtedly he would have been killed had he been dragged much further. As it was I saved his life, aid he there and then made a vow that if ever I needed help I should have it, and it should be given me freely. It was the mempry of this which prompted me to interview him • rind as •I now stood before him, I reminded him of his promise, and claimed his halo. He remembered it all, and seemed quite willing to redeem his promise, only waiting for me to state the manner in which he could help rue. He was not pre- pared thoughlfor what I had determined to ask. As I stated my business,—that I had come to ask for the loan of his Coronation robes and coronet, together with his ticket of admission to the Abbey—l thought he would have collapsed. He looked at rue as though he saw in me a victim of malignant smallpox, or as though I were a desperate burglar standing before him. After a moment or two he seemed to resover some what and thus I got a chance of showing him that what I asked was not as preposterous as it seemed. I pointed out that as he could not use these things for the occasion himself, owing to his a-mident it would h 3 a pity for the opportunity of taking his place to be thrown away: and that the strong resem- blance which I bore to him would secure me against detection. Then I made the most aecessary statement of all, namely, that I would give my word of honour that everything should be returned to him safely. At laqt I thought I saw sigrs of him wavering, and, putting in all I knew, as a last plea, I was gratified to hear him say I might have them on the conditions that I retcrned them safely, and ti at I came and gave him a descriptive account of all the proceedings. This readily nrornised to do, and T left the cl'arnber delighted. Whatever strange thoughts my wife had before of my sanity, she now seemed to have made up mind that she was correct in her suppositions; for as I entered the house and placed the gorgeous robes belonging to the Earl of Kirby in a safe pla-as, and secured the coronet within the precincts of my little safe, she seemed thunderstruck.

I said not a word vet, for I wanted everthing to ha perfectly secret. If I failed,no one should know of the aGempt. If I succeeded, it was nother matter alto- gether. I had secured from Lord Kirby • all the necessary iaformation about cabs and hotel. My plan was to , impersonate the Earl, and thus secure a seat at the I historic function in the Abbey. The evening before the Coronation arrived and found me with every detail arranged. Punctually at 7.45 my train drew up at St. Pancras Station. Hailing' a cab, I gave the cabby instructions to drive me to my hotel, which I had chosen far enough away from the haunts of the aristoc- racy. I had only one travelling bag, and no one dreamed that it coatained the necessm y adjuncts of a real Peer of the Realm. Aftar some refreshment in my apart men:, I beihn to overhaul the borrowed plumage. First I put on the long flowing robes, and looked at myself in the glass. Yes, there I was, every inch a Peer! Then I I took up the coronet, placed it on my head, and again surveyed myself in the mirror. Yes, everything seemed all right, but—there was too much brilliancy about the coronet. I took it off and looked at it I thought as ' much. Lord Kirby was no better than anyone else. nstn lad of the re-al thing it was only an imitation. And I - , yet, how could I expect that he would trust me, a com- parative stranger, with the coronet, if it was the real thing. But no matter; it would evidently have done service for the real Earl, if he had bcm well enough to attend; so I a spuriouS Earl, could not grumble if a part of my credentials were spurious too. On Coronation morning I awoke blithe and happy. One of the first things I did was to examine all my , credentials, and make sure that nothing was missing to • pass me off as a Pear of the Realm. I About half an hour before the proper time, I arrived at the Peers' entrance to the Abbey in ma ordinary four- wheeler. As cabby jumped off the box and openc I the , cab door, he seemed amazed. I had entircl the cab as an ordinary individual with a travellinp bag. I was ' emerging attired as a Peer of the Realm 1. don't know what he was about to say, for just 59 he opened his mouth to speak, I whisp Ned into his ear my arrange- ments for returning to my hotel. Then I commenced to walk in a dignified wanner towards the door where Lord Kirby said 1 wrg to enter. Like the cabman, those in charge of this entrance, seemed to look upon meas a strange individual if not as an imposter. But as soon as I presented my ticket of admission signed by the Earl Marshall himsalf, this look of incredulity disappeared and I was allowed to entar, with probably the only thought ca^t after me that I

Towards evening I gathered together the borrowed articles of the Earl o: Kirby, and wended my way towards the Castle. This time I was ushered in with more respect than before, for the servants seemeo to understand that I wa.s on visiting terms with the Earl. As I entered the room where the Earl sat, I saw that his foot must be con- siderably better for he was laughing to himself. I supposed, as he caught sight of me, that he was laughing over the fun of my aeventure,even before I had related anything to him. Sit down, my dear fellow, and tell me all." I accepted his invitation and remarked that I had brought back all his property in perfect order. "I knew you would, or I would never have lent them to you; and now, pray, go on with your story." began my narrative, and put in my best efforts to make it interesting. Net a detailed was missed. I even lent additional colour to some things, in my zeal to interest the Earl. When I got to that portion where the old Pear came upon the scene, he ,aughed uproar- ously and T was afraid he would break a blood vessel : but he didn't. " Why, man," said he, "it must have been the Marquis of Mendon, the old schoolfellow of my father's. And didhe really say you were a chip off the old block ? Just like him. Go on with your story." I went on again, telling him of my excuse to get rid of the old roan, by limping and feigaing great pain as we emerged from the Abbey. He laughed louder than ever. Just when his laughter was at its highest, I heard another suppressed laugh from the direction of a screen at the farther end of the room. • Simultaneously with tbo laugh I saw the screen move and to my amazement, or, , stepped from its shadow the old Marquis himself. There he was, like Lord Kirby doubled up with laughing. Now I began to see what cur.:Nl the Earl's excessive laughter. I knew my narra- tive was of sufficient interest to cause some little fun, but I had not thought it possessed the merit of bring- ing forth such a great amount of merriment. Now I saw it all. .

To help me somewhat in my deductior the Earl began to tell me all that had happened after I left him. When I visited the Castle on the first occasion I had not been gone many minutes when his father's old friend the Marquis of Mendon, called on him and learned for the first time of his accident. Toe Earl told him of my visit, and my remarkable request. The Ma-qd;s W. 19 astounded at my presumption, but being a man %Nilo wig always fond of fim he fancied he saw in the incidentthe element of a good joke. Together they planned the rest arranging that the old man should hurry back in time to hear the account of my adventure. And there they were. A Marquis, an Earl, sad a common clerk. could not help laughing myself, for their merriment was infectious. t last,when they were getting over it some- what, I began to think that I, a common clerk, should begin to show some signs of leaving such exalted com- pany and betake myself to ray proper sphere. Just as I was about to say something to that effect, and move towards the door, the old Marquis steppcd up to me, and, placing his hand on my shoulder, said: " My dear fellow, f want a private secretary ; I have I been so pleased with your adventure, and your display of resource, that, if you'll agree, I'll engage you at a ' handsome salary." Needless to say, I agreed ; and when I went home to my wife, instead of her thinking I had a suicidal ten- dency, she told me that she had always thought I had a greater amount of brains than most people, and that now she was sure of it.

FIRST Dl%lsxoN.

It is really high time that some definite decision be made concerning the side upon which traffic is to over- take these tram cars. Common sense suggests the near side, since by passing on the off side, there is always the danger of encountering another car proceeding from the opposite direction. It seems. however, that there is no absolute rule upon the subject, so that, from a legal standpoint, the tram should be overtaken on its off side in the same manner as other and steerable traffic.

It is a thousand pities that motor 'bus service was not seriously attempted sooner than has been the case. Had this been done and vehicles proved successful,there is no doubt that we should have been spared the present outiageous condition of many of our main roads which are cut up by the train lines, constituting, as they do,a continual menace to the safety of the public. The • average electric tramcar travels a great deal too fast, considering that it is unable to get out of the way of I any other vehicle. The speed allowed varies from ten • to twelve miles an hour, according to the district ; but actual timing experiments show that the average speed I generally works out at something between sixteen and I eighteen miles per hour. Mr. Moffat Ford drew public attention to the subjc 3t, about eighteen months ago when he summoned four drivers of electric cars for ex- ceeding their legal speed limit and the police should see to it that this limit is not exceeded. Such vigilaece would be better directed than in being employed, as it too frequently is, in trapping motorists over deserted stretches of country roads.

Motorists who understand their engines and are duly careful of the mechanism cannot understand the inexcni- able and apparently limitless ignorance of many who own and Ste sr—one cannot say "drive "—expensi'Ve and valuable gars solely by virtue of having paid the neces- sary five shillings for a license. To them the construe- “on of the wonderful piece of mechanism they think they control is a sealed mystery : and they are quite content to allow it to remain so. We have just be3n show a a pair of gear wheels which have been in use barely three weeks, and the teeth are so cut and chipped t'at they are now quite useless. It is not a case of soft matm ial hang bc3n used ; they have simply been haclui to pi. 3es by careless gear-changing. As the driver still s ma to be undecided in his own mind whether he should de-clutch before or after shifting the change-speed lever, the condition of the pinions is not remarkable.

One would have supposed that a grown and presum- ably intelligent person would have wanted to know eloritly what happened to the mechanism when that lever was moved. Having satisfied himself upon this point, it should then have been as instinctive to him to de-clut^'. before attempting to change speed as it is to a cyclist to back-pedal when he wants to slow-up. People used to talk about" leak aing" to back-pedal. They might as well have advised learning to breathe, or to put one foot before the other when desiring to walk. In the rime way there should rellly be no learning the vse of a clutch pedal oneo its function has been grasped

The fact of the matter is there are many persons who ought not to be allowed the luxury of a csr. Invin- cible ignorance cannot control a motor,and so, one day, the ex' ,cted happens. This does not perhaps matter very greatly es far as the owner is concernc 1, for the patt3rn of the ordinary fool was standardised long ago and Nature turns it out by the gross; but it is lament- able to think of delieste and expensive mechenism being de -t royed.

Tim risk of being struck by lightning is five times greater in the country than in cities.

POIILTRY manure is very rich in all the fertilising elements, but especially so in nitrogen.

A Nag glass, described by M. Emile Touchet, is very transparent to invisible chemical rays. In simultaneous photographs of part of the constella- tion Lyra, a lens of this glass shewed 819 stars, while one of ordinary Jena glass shewed but 351.

Arsenic is übiquitous, says Science Siftings. It is found in our organism; indeed, all our organs contain some arsenic, which we eliminate when we cut our nails and hair, both of which contain con- siderable quantities of arsenic.

IN THE TIME TO COME.

When the transformation of cities by electric power and light is completed, we may expect the air to be practically as pure as that of the country. It is estimated that the carbonic acid exhaled yearly by the people of a city of 2,000,000 is about 450,000 tons, but that this is less than 3 I,er cent, of that from fuel combustion.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY STARS TWINKLE.

Stars twinkle on account of their light reaching us through variously heated and moving currents of air. Hence much twinkling of the stars foretells bad weather, because it denotes that there are various aerial currents of different temperatures and densities, producing atmospheric disturbance.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
COLOUR BLINDNESS.

The most common form of colour blindness is an inability to distinguish red. In one year-1904 thirty-four officers and would-be officers of the British mercsntile marine service failed on their colour tests, twenty-three being red-blind, and the remainder unable to distinguish green. The 4,600 candidates for certificates were also submitted to the fora vision tests, and twenty-two of them failed to distinguish the form of the object sub. mitted.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
LIFE ON THE MOON.

Professor Max Haushofer, of the University of Berlin, believes that life, animal and vegetable, is not •xtinct on the moon. Water probably existi underground, and the last of the Selenites may dwell in pits, craters, and caverns, fly or move by means of wings, and live upon worms. They dare not face the sunlight as we do, and their eyes are like those of bats or owls. It would be interesting to know their ideas about ourselves.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
WINDS AND THE TEMPERATURE.

In an article on the "Temperature of the Mr,* which Mr. William Marriott, secretary of the Royal Meteorological Society, contributes to Knowlea'fie as the first of a series on "Practical Meteorology," is a summary of the effects of the prevailing winds of Great Britain on the temperature: N. winds depress the temperature throughout the year. N.E. winds do the same, except in summer, when their effect is small.

E. winds lower the temperature very much in winter, and generally raise it in summer. S.E. winds do nearly the same, but less markedly in winter.

S. winds raise the temperature much in winter, but scarcely affect it in summer. S.W. winds do nearly the same. W. winds decidedly raise the temperature in winter, and lower it in summer.

N.W. winds lower the temperature generally, but most in summer.

ALUMINIUM AND PLATING.

Aluminium does not readily lend itself to plating, because the plated metal tends quickly to scale off, and the defect has been attributed to the micro- scopically thin film of oxide which forms on the surface of the aluminium. One method of dealing with the metal is to immerse it in soluble fluorides, together with some free hydrofluoric acid; and thus not only to remove the oxide film, but to prepare the surface of the aluminium for the reception of a plate of other metal by roughening its surface. The aluminium is then quickly rinsed and immersed in a bath of zinc and aluminium sulphates, and while in the bath a film of zinc is deposited on it by the ordinary methods of electro- plating. Other metals may now be plated on the zinc. An electrolytic film of gold will, however, disapear in the zinc, so that if it is required to give a gold plating to the aluminium-zinc surface it must further be coated with copper.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
FOG-DISPERSION. EQUIPMENTS.

The first recorded experiments tending towards the scientific dispersion of fog, smoke, and fumes were made by Professor Tyndall in 1870, who found that when dust-laden air came in contact with a hot body, such as a candle or red-hot poker, a space was instantly cleared of the foreign articles. Ten years later, Lord Rayleigh turned his attention to the problem, and, by substituting a lamp of ice for the hot poker, found that the temperature of the ice caused a dust-free space to be formed, the dark plane of which was bordered by bright fringes of dusty air of great sharpness. Here the experi- ments rested, remarks a popular scientific con- temporary, until 1863. when Professors Lodge and Clarke, after numerous tOsts, concluded that there emanated from the hot or cold body a molecular bombardment which drove the dust away from it. Accordingly, Sir Oliver Lodge decided to make a practical test of the dispersion of fog by electricity. 'To accomplish this task an insulated wire was led from the laboratory of the Birmingham University to a Hag-staff on the roof. The wire terminated in a number of fine points, and as widely separated as possible. The base of the wire was connected to the positive pole of a high-tension electric machine. The opposite or complementary pole was laid to the earth, the system resembling very much a send- ing-off station for wireless telegraphy. When a dense fog had enveloped the building with a cloak so thick that the eye could scarcely penetrate it for more than a foot or two, the professor and his colleagues mounted the roof. On a given signal the electrical energy was literally poured from the elevated points into the surrounding fog. The result was as successful as it was astounding. The fog cleared away in the immediate vicinity of the points, leaving a space absolutely clear.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SUN'S RADIANT HEAT.

It is interesting to read some of the actual calculations made by Professor Poynting as to the real solar temperature. The method used was gimplicity itself, though peculiarly difficult in practice. A well-known law describes the definite relationship that exists between the temperature of a hot body and the heat it radiates. In the case of the sun, therefore, all that has to be done is to measure the heat received by a measured portion of the earth's surface, calculating what fraction this is of the whole surface the sun could heat at the same distance. Proceeding in this way, Dr. Punting arrives at the

ENORMOUS TEMPERATURE Of 11,250.0 deg. F., eight times as far removed from freozing-point as is a bright red furnace. Fortu- nately, says the St. James's Gazette, this figure has been wonderfully closely borne out by a converse method. In this it is shewn that, with the sun at about 11,000.0 deg. F., the average temperature of the earth should be about 7571eg. F.—which is pretty nearly the case. In the same way Mars is found to possess a mean of over 100 deg. of frost, a fact perhaps in itself sufficient to dispel much pro- bability of its being inhabited, at least by "life" such as it is in this world. But these points by no means exhaust the variety of conclusions to be drawn from the fact of the sun's radiant heat. One of the

CHARACTERISTICS OF ETHER WAVES, such as those of light and heat, is that they pro- duce a distinct pressure in the direction they travel. True, in the case of the earth this outward pressure from the sun is barely 70,000 tons, a mere flea-bite compared with th vast gravitational attraction towards the elm. hut imagine the earth reduced in size many thousimi times. This wav9 pressure will diminish far !Imre slowly in propor- tion to the decrease of gravitation, such that a time will come when the two may be in equilibrium or the outward pressure may even be the greater. Slid.' a conclusion has led Dr. Vernon Boys to give a highly ingenious EXPLANATION OF THE COMET'S TAIL.

Fine dust is supposed to be produced by explo- sions in the group of meteors which constitute a comet, and thus, though the meteors themselves may be drawn towards the sun, these dust particles, being small enough for gravitation to be neutralised, would be driven outwards, thus forming the familiar fanlike comet shapes. One might even infer that, speaking geyerallyt, perfect equilibrium being so difficult to maintain, the EMI is ever occupied in clearing its own system by either drawing paiticles to itself or driving them into outer space.

MOTOR NOTES.

GARDENING ENCOURAGED.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
The Work of the Stockton Heath

!Garden Society.

In encouraging the inhabitants of the village to take a pride in making their gardens as attractive as possible the Stockton Heath Garden Society is fulfilling a use- ful purpose. Satisfactory progress was reported at the Society's annual meeting which was held in the Church Room on Monday evening under the presidency of Mr. C. Plinston.

_ The annual report presented by the honorary secretary (Mr. W. A. Timperley) stated that the society seemed to make satisfactory progress every year. The number of members was 193, as compared with 102 the yea? pre- vious. The annual show was held in August, and was a great success, the exhibits being of a very high order and the number of exhibitors larger. The usual visits to the gentlemen's summer gardens in the district had been well atended, and highly appreciated by all. Several papers had been read during the past year upon various flowers. etc., and a lecture had also been delivered by Mr. Wm. Nield. under the auspices of the Cheshire County Council. There were three more lectures to be given by that gentleman. The attendance at some of the meetings had been very disappointing, He drew their attention to the monthly meeting, and said the object was to keep the members more in touch with one another. (Applause). On the motion of Mr. J. Moore, seconded by Mr. E. Vernon, the report was adopted. Mr. T. R. Westbrook, treasurer, reported that the year commenced with a balance in hand of £3 10s. 7d. The total receipts amounted to £26 6s. 4d. and after paying expenses there was a balance in hand of 7s.

OFFICIALS ELECTED.

_ On the motion of Mr. J. Ryan seconded by Mr. J. Moore, Mr. Vere B. Davies was unanimously re-elected president. Mr. J. Ryan proposed the re-election of Mr. West- brook, as treasurer for the coming year, Mr. A. Broady seconding. The Treasurer said he had come there with the inten- tion of asking them to relieve him of the office, and he would be pleased to hand over the papers to anyone. The Chairman said they had got every good treasurer and he hoped that Mr. Westbrook would change his decision.

Mr. Westbrook said he had been thinking matters over, and as they had such a small balance in hand, he did not like to leave it. He would therefore accept the position for a further twelve months. (Hear, hear and applause.) Mr. Timperley resigning, it was decided to appoint Mr. Fawcett to the secretaryship. With r`eference to the committee, the Chairman said that at the last meeting it was decided to have an in- crease in the number. He thought it would be desir- able if that was done. The present number was seven. Mr. Ryan said the work of the committee was most required on a show day, and it was the duty of the com- mittee to be there on that day, and help the secretary. During past years there had only been one or two com- mittee men present. After some discussion, it was decided to increase the number to twelve members, and the following were appointed :—Messrs. E. Vernon, J. Ryan,C. Plinston, J. Moore, A. Broad/, W. H. Woodcock. A. J Willett, S, Booth, F. Anslow, T. Eden, W. A. Tirnperley, and F. Timperley.

PENKETH.

PARISH COUNCIL AND COUNTY COUNOIL SCHOLAR- SHIM—A meeting of the Parish Council was held in the Wesleyan Schools on Monday, Mr. Richard Rout- ledge presiding. Messrs. :James Ditchfleld, Isaac Ashcroft, and Mr. E. Foster (clerk) were present. The clerk stated that he had not yet received an acknow- ledgment from Lord Derby or Colonel Pilkington, M.P. in answer to his inquiries re the appointment of a local county magistrate. The Clerk had obtained the cost per lamp for the lighting of Penketh, which was as follows, viz., 19 lamps, £2 2s. 6d. each per annum, and the new lamps recently erected in Sankey road, £3 Bs. These amounts, added to the lamplighter's salary, totalled approximately £7O per annum.—The Chairman alluded to the many scholarships, &c., now open for competition amongst day school scholars, and asked the clerk to inquire whether the press notification was the only method or system the County Council Education Committee or Sub-committee had of advertising them. If so, he thought a recommendation should go from the council, or steps be taken by them to let the parents know the particulars of such scholarships so that their children could be presented for examination. Nowadays they could ill afford to allow anything in the nature of education to slip by, and he advised that they, as a council should take full advantage of everything that presented itself for the benefit of the rising generation lin the village. The suggestion was approved.

RUNCORN SAVINGS BANK.

The sixty-second annual meeting of trustees and managers was held on Tuesday, under the presidency of Mr. J. R. Frith. During the year there had been 4,150 transactions. The deposits totalled £12,218 118. sd. and the withdrawals £12,350 16s. 3d. The amount due to depositors was £41,609 18s. 6d. There was a surplus of assets over liabilities of £l6B ss. 2d. The actuary (Mr. W. Handley), in his report, referred to the com- petition of joint stock commercial banks with savings banks by receiving deposits of Is. and upwards, and suggested that the restriction at present upon savings banks limiting the deposits to £5O annually should be removed. The report was adopted.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

NORTHERN LEAGUE.

First Division.

Warrington v. Hull.

This was a popular fixture at Warrington, and 5,000 people attended. The weather was excellent and a fast, exciting game resulted. Hull were short of Taylor, the County full back, and played Jenkins in his place, while Warrington turned out as selected. Mr. Renton of Hunslet was the referee, and the teams were: Warrington: Hallam, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Brookes, and Kenyon, threequarters ; Hockenhall and D. Davies, half backs; Boardman, Preston, Shugars, Belton, Thomas, Jolley, Harmer, and Swift, forwards. Hull: Jenkins, full back; Freear, Cook, Goodfellow and Darcey, threequarters ; Hall and Salt, half backs ; Ritson, Farrel, J. Lewis, Harrison, J. H. Lewis, Rhodes, Kilburn, and Wade, forwards. Warrington lost the toss and started against a strong breeze. Mid-field play ensued for a minute or two, until a long kick carried the game to the Warrington end. Brookes fielded nicely, but allowed Cook to charge the return and a try at the corner was the result. The home team at once forced the pace, and carried play to the Hull hue. Here Hockenhall ran strongly, and parted to Davies, who got over in a good position, Preston kicking a goal. Fine football followed, both sides combining effectively. Freear followed up well once, but Kenyon saved the situation. Hull were hotly attacking when Fish cleverly stole a pass meant for Freear, and put in a superb dodgy run nearly the length of the field. A penalty sent \Warrington back, and Hall securing from the pack, tricked Hallam and scored near the posts. Goodfellow added a goal, and at half-time Hull were leading by 8 points to 5. Restarting, Warrington made good use of the wind, and Jenkins was soon called upon. Isherwood collared him at centre, and Brookes getting the ball at his toes, dribbled right under the posts, Fish placing a goal, and giving Warrington a lead of two points. Vigorous play by both teams followed, but Warrington were mostly on the attack. For some time nothing accrued, but in the last quarter Davies and Isherwood passed across to Fish, who made a dazzling run down touch, beating Freear and Jenkins, and scoring near the posts. The scorer missed an easy goal shot. Just on time fast following up by Hull got the ball past Hallam, and Cook won the race for possession, and scored far out. Good- fellow tried at goal, but missed, and Warrington were returned winners by two goals and three tries (13 points), to Hull one goal and three tries (11 points).

BATLEY v. OLDHAM. At Batley. Playing downhill first half, Oldham monopolised the attack, and after very narrow escapes, Batley's line was crossed by Dixon, Fergusson placing the goal. Batley then went away to the other end, and with a little more care and better judgment a couple of tries at the least should have been scored. At the interval Batley were behind to the extent of live points. The second part opened with a couple of accidents, one to Shackleton and the other to Lewis. The latter proved to be the more seriously damaged, and had to leave the field with a broken collar-bone. Batley failed miserably to rise to the occasion, and tries were scored against them by McLean and Carpenter, Oldham ulti- mately winning by 1 goal 3 tries (11 points), to Batley nil.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. HELENS v. SWINTON.

Playing at home, St. Helens had the best of a well- contested game, and won by 5 points to 3. About 4,000 people witnessed the game. With the wind behind them Swinton had rather the better of the exchanges in the opening half, and just before the interval, Wallwork scored an unimproved try for them. On resuming, Swinton became dangerous, but St. Helens tackled well, and Briers dribbling half the length of the field, Both- well got over and equalised. A little later Traynor placed a goal for St. Helens.

===== 0002601_19050121_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
WAKEFIELD TRINITY v. HALIFAX.

At Wakefield, before about 3,000 people. The ground wa.s in a very heavy condition. The visitors were on several occasions penalised for informalities in the scrimmages, and Metcalfe and Newbould each placed a goal. Afterwards the home backs indulged in some pretty passing, which culminated in Emmerson getting over, Metcalfe missing the goal from near the touch- line side. Near the interval Newbould put in a foolish kick, and the ball going to H. Morley, this player got over the line without any opposition, Wedgewood fail- ing at goal. Half-time :—Wakefield, 2 goals I try (7 points) ; Halifax, I try (3 points.) Result :—Wakefield, 3 goafs 1 try (9 points) ; Halifax, I try (3 points.) LEIGH v. BROUGHTON RANGERS.

Visiting Leigh, the Rangers won by a goal and two tries to nil. In the first half, from a cross-kick by Harry, Wilson ran forty yards, and scored for the Rangers, but the try was not improved. Leigh pressed repeatedly in the second half, but had three tries dis- allowed. Nearing the end, the Rangers broke away,and Harry scored, Harris improving. THE REREFEE AND THE CROWD. Feeling ran very high among the Leigh spectators, through Mr. Dance, of Morecambe, the referee, dis- allowing Leigh three apparently fair tries. After the match, policemen and officials, observing the threaten- ing attitude of the crowd, who hooted continuously, escorted the refereree to the pavilion,kept him there an hour, and then distracted the crowd's attention by send- ing men in a cab one way, whilst the referee was hurried across country to a tramcar. SALFORD V. BRADFORD. In fine weather, this interesting match took place at Weaste, in the presence of 6,000 people. From the Salford side Lomas and Thomas were absent. Dribbling and passing between Francis, Turner, Greenwood and Connell resulted in the last-named dashing over at the corner. Mosby failed at goal. A change of hostilities gave Salford their opportunity, and Bedford made honours easy by sooting a try, which Richards did not improve upon. Before the interval a bout of passing between the Bradford backs ended in ,Dechan running in, Marsden's kick being abortive. Half-time :—Brad- ford two tries (6 points), to Salford one try (3 points). On resuming, Bradford attained superiority forward,and Eagers scored a brilliant try, the kick at goa being unsuccessful. Bedford made a good effort on behalf of Salford, who, however, were mainly placed on the defensive, and a smart run by Eagers gave Mosby a Clear try between the posts. Marsden took the place and landed a goal. Result:—Bradford one poal and four tries (14 points), to Salford one try (3 points).

WIDNES v. LEEDS. At Widnes. Widnes did good work in the early stages, and their opponents had a share of defensive Work to do. But the Leeds men came throu4h the ordeal well, and ultimately lAittlewood had a chance With a penalty shot, and this time landed a goal. Sub- sequently the Leeds full back had another chance, and kicked his second penalty goal, which left the visitors With a four points lead at the interval. In the second half the home team had a chance to reduce the Leees advantage, but Tilley missed an easy penalty shot at goal. There was a seeming inability on both sides to score tries, but late in the game Aspey scored for Widnes owing to mulling by the Leeds backs. Tilley just failed at goal, and thus Widnes, who would have won by* a point had this goal been kicked, lost by a similar margin, the final score being—Leeds, 2 goals (4 points) ; Widnes, 1 try (3 points).

HULL KINGSTON ROVERS v .WIGAN. One of the largest crowds this season witnessed then above match at Craven-street, fully 8,000 people being present. The Rovers had the enjoyment of a brisk breeze at the start, and Barry contributed a nice run, Which only failed at Mason. There was some hard scrimmaging, and the ball came out on the home side. Gordon paved the way for a magnificent try by Medley. Carmichael failed at goal. Hopkins was beaten by Phipps in an effort to get the ball to Leytham after this, and Barry also joined in the movement. This culminatbd in the ball again reaching 'Medley, who put on top speed and raced over with another clever try. Carmichael again failed to add. The heavy ground had churned up badly by this time, and correct handling was impossible. West, however, took the ball from Phipps nicely, but was bowled over by Mason. Near tbe end Leytham dribbled cleverly, and went over the line with an eft?3, try, Mason landing a fine goal. Result Kingston Rovers two tries (0 points), to Wigan one goal and one try (5 points).

COUNTY CHAMPIONSHIP.

Cumberland v. Lancashire.

This county match was played at Whitehaven on Monday, under exceedingly unfavourable conditions, a piercing cold and strong south-easterly wind sweeping the ground, which was somewhat hard. About 1,500 spectators were present. Lancashire started, and Wedgwood scored in the first few minutes, Eagers failed at goal. This was the only score in the first half. Play was even until the interval, when Cumberland led by one try to Lancashire nothing. Lancashire objected to resuming owing to the hardness of the ground, but the referee ruled against the objection, and the visitors returned to the field amidst ironical cheers. The home forwards rushed to the Lancashire line. Wedgwood scoring under the posts. The same player also con- verted. Cumberland continued to have the best of the play, and Austin scored the third try, Eagers failing at goal. Final score: Cumberland, one goal and three tries (11 points) ; Lancashire, nil.

CHESHIRE JUNIOR LEAG UE. ROBERTSON'S V. WIDNES ROVERS.

At Latchford on Saturday last. The home team kicked off before a strong wind, and play immediately went into the visitors 25. Even play followed, Bailey making a good run along the line, but was well tackled. From midfield the ball was sent up with a long kick, and the visitors scored a try. The goal was then added. Play after this was well in the visitors half, Green making a good run, but was pushed into touch by the last man. The home forwards were completely out- weighing the visitors, although they were only five in number. Play was still in the visitors' half, when from a penalty Ashurst kicked a good goal. Half-time : Widnes Rovers, 5 points; Robertson's, 2 points. Resum- ing, the homesters with the wind behind them gave the Widnes defence some hard work, and after rather one- sided play, Hunter from the base of the pack, dropped a goal. Play still kept well up the field, the home for- wards operating strongly against their opponents, and the backs playing a much better and open game. Hunter again receiving from the pack, ran through the Rovers and scored a good try at the corner. The kick at goal failed. Result :—Robe rtson's, 7 points; Widnes Rovers, 5 points.

Steamship's Second Officer Cured by

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills.

Mr. William Gledhill, second officer of the steam- ship "Stepney" of Goole, describes the Paralytic Stroke which laid him helpless, in these graphic terms "We welt, mooring alongside Goole wharf at 2.30 a.m., when I felt a shock as though I kad been stabbed in the baek of the head. I pain shot through my left arm, down my side and into my foot. I thought I had been stabbed, but, as I will explain, )ad been str- 'th Paralysis. I don't look - like it? No, but if it

hadn't been' for Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, I should have been still

Paralysed. "When the Stroke

ran through me,l dropped, but getting hold of the railings with my right hand, I managed to crawl with one hand and one

knee to my terth. I remained helpless for an

hour, when f heard the

, second engineer come into the galley to make tea,. and I called to him

for7help. .18 thaugh had been stabbod,' "I was supported to. my home, which was is Mr. Gledhill's diwcriptiow fortunately close by— sf a Paralytic Stroke which 2, Jame-street, Barge Df.Willia'iiJePiU,oetef Dock Side, Goole—on the shoulders of two men. I have a wife and children to keep ! When I had accepted my fate as a life-long cripple, a book left at the house described the cure of a Paralysed man by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. I tried thee., with wonderful results. After the very first box the Palsy in my arm ceased, and I have never had it since. Up to that time the arm had twitched all night long, and my foot also. After three boxes I was able to get out of bed and walk to my chair with- out assistance. "After I had started Dr. Williams' Pink Pills I took no more ordinary medicine. I am well and strong and following the sea just as well as if I had never been Paralysed." " You feel convinced that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills all nothing else, brought this about ?" " Fully couvinced," said Mr. Gledhill. " Occasion- ally now, when I feel a little out of sorts, after being a long time on my feet, I take one or two of the pills. I am right again almost directly. I never go to sea with- out them. 1 can move my left arm now just as freely as my right. I have used these pills for one of my sons, a delicate, bloodless lad of 16,apprenticed to a plumber. Since taking the pills the lad has develoded a healthy appetite, and is progressing apace." HOCKEY.

STOCKTON HEATH v. LATCHFORD.

These teams tried conclusions on the ground of the first-named on Saturday. Latchford won the toss and played with wind and slope in their favour. The' visitors pressed vigorously for some time, but their efforts lacked finish end the home goal was never in serious danger. At length Stockton Heath took up the running, but with no result, Hallows presenting a very sound defence. While the home team were pressing hard Cliffe broke away in brilliant style, running over half the length of the field, but the Stockton Heath goal-keeper cleverly diverted his final shot. The resultine corner was easily cleared, and mid-field play brought about half-time with the score sheet still blank. Changing over, play was even for some time, but at length Stockton Heath got in the visitors' quarters. Their forward play was very ragged and slow, and though they forced several corners a score never seemed likely. The Latchford forwards on the other hand were much more dangerous when in possession, and several times caused the home defence anxious moments, but they were safely kept out of the Stockton circle, and the game looked likely to be drawn without score, when R. Pier- point getting hold from a scramble close to the Latchford goal ran through, and in spite of the attentions of three opponents succeeded in safely netting the ball, thus giving the home side the lead. With ten minutes to go, Latchford played up desper- ately, and had hard lines on at least three occasions,but the home team full backs played a cool and confident game, and the equalizer never came. Excitement ran high when the home goal-keeper was forced to give a penalty corner close on time, but the opportunity was not grasped, and time found Stockton Heath victors by one goal to nil.

Diagram 53 shows the object balls so situated that a very simple cannon is on when a player is in hand. The easiest way of playing a cannon or in fact any stroke is, however, seldom the best way if any thought is to be given to the after-position. If the positions of the red and white balls in the diagram were reversed the simple ball-to-ball cannon played slowly would undoubtedly be the game, as by such a stroke the red could be dribbled to the pocket, and pro- vided care were taken not to pot the red, a pot or an in-off would be left for the next stroke, either ,gif which could be played in such a way as to leave further position. When, however, the white is the second object-ball, as shown in the diagram, bidden dangers lurk in the simple ball-to-ball cannon. A shade too much strength and there is always the possibility of the white finding the pocket; and even though this danger Oe avpicled, .119 w. easily my the white ball leave

ANSWERS TO CORRESPONDENTS. " Player " (London.)—Stevenson has not yet made a break of 500 this season but Dawson has made a 515 break. A. Jones (Chorley.)—lf the red (on the spot) is finked into any pocket it counts as a pot off the spot, and if before the fluke it had already been potted off the spot, it should after the fluke be placed on the centre spot. E. Harding (Derby).—lf a bail is knocked off the table no stroke made by the striker can count. Instead the stroke counts two to the non-striker. " Billiards " (Edinburgh).—lvory balls are generally either Indian or African ivory. Mammoth ivory which is found in the Siberian Steppes and is many thousands of years old is of no use whatever for billiard balls, as a ball made of this ivory very quickly becomes quite untrue. Bonzoline balls are made in America, but come into this country free of any duty. Crystalate Balls are a British production, being made at Tonbridge, near London. A. E. Topping (Manchester).—You cannot play good billiards on a cloth that has been turned. I went into the question of nap in the chapter on side. You men- tion your ball running off so much. This is doubtless caused by the ball being untrue and not by side. If you have any crystalate bslls at your club try these one ' night and let me know the result. RISO LEVI

LOCAL HARRIERS.

Cross Country Running by Warrington

Teami

I Warririkton A.C. had across-country race with North I Manchester Harriers on Saturday and gained an easy victory. There was a muster of 30 runners-20 War- rington, and 10 North Manchester. LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENT. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matters intended for publication must be written on side ortly.of the paper and sent in, if possible, not later than Thursday.

Recent Warrington Eisteddfod. Sir,—Will you allow me to call attention to an injustice—unintentional I have no doubt on the part of the officials--to one of the competitors at the recent Warrington Eisteddfod? I induced my grand-daughter Maid Ethel Chapman, of Stretford, but formerly of Warrington, where she received the groundwork of her musical education, to enter for the pianoforte solo con- test for young people under 18. She is barely 15. She entered under her own initials "M. E. C." Among the three called on to perform before the public in the afternoon besides the two who were awarded prizes was "F. E. C." and a girl stepped up and played, but the adjudicator said he was much disappointed as she played so nicely in the morning. Judge of my surprise on reading your report of the pro- ceedings on the following Saturday to find that "M. E. C." was intended. I learn from the secretary, Mr. R. Cook, that there was no " E. C."; that my granddaughter was one of the selested three, and that inconsequence of this slight mistake,we had the mortifi- cation of realizing that we had sat in the hall while her place was usurped by a girl whose initials were not " F. E. C." and who had entered under another nam de p/uw.e. I don't wonder at her nervousnessat the piano. I know the matter cannot be rectified now, but, though 'late in the day, I ask for the insertion of this in justice to the wronged competitor, who has many friends in Warringtom—Yours, Scc.,

W. E. STOUT.

January 20th.

PHYLLIS THE ELUSIVE.

[By Godfrey Dean.] lOn the Colour of Ribbons.

Since Phyllis will not marry me, if she would oily leave me in peace !—or that state of mind which must pass for such. But no. She must for ever send me notes (indeed, every other day)—curious, little things, by whose delicate fragrance I could, blindfolded, pick them I ' from the post-bag—indefinitely c orded, allusive, and always requiring explanation. And one must find out what she means! But enough of it. She shall trifle with me no longer. Only to-day she wrote:— 1 . . . " Perhaps I would be calling in the course of two or three years ?" (As I have often had occasion to tell Phyllis, her language is extravagant. I She means to-morrow.) " Her cousin had paid them I a visit. Had not seen him for three years—since he was twenty-one. . , Wonderfully improved, ' Handsome fellow now 1" It didn't interest me, of course. Nor possibilities. lam grown indifferent these days. Yet I'll see her once more—and then to the shell of nay reserve, forgetting there's such a place as Cadogan-square— yes, and Phyllis, too! What a time the footman was opening the door. Phyllis was weaning, the gr.wn I liked her best in; though Phyllisjiaina you., in anything is—well ,Phylli ' She smiled at ma, but ,her saitcharLes' found ins i. passive. site eXpPealedAer eyes wide with - der - tihyllis is —nothing if nat.elusive. • " Exactly,'" I replied, in a matter-of-fact tone. " Not going into the country, then?" " Did I say so'?" " Perhaps not, but you don't tell me everything." " And you won't listen to what I have to tell you,' complained. " Change," she quoted, "is the first law of nature." Phyllis paused, sugar-tongs in hand. "One or two ?" Now this was absurd. If Phyllis has given me tea once—

But I am not to be seduced from my purpose. Henceforth I am negative—quite negative—towards ~her in everything. "As you pl'.ase," I said. Phyllis put two larcps in my cup. I looked inquiringly at her. "You left it to me," she remarked. I'm afraid Phyllis lacks balance. Nevertheless I took the tea. "Is it sweet enough? " I made no reply. She raised her dainty head, and put down her. cup. .Phyllis is charming even when she frowns. "What is it?" tasked. "Nothing." "You frowned," I sai.{l. "Did I?" "Yes. Why not face the fact?" "Well, then if I did I'll admit it." "So far, good. Why did you?" " You.are serious." "I repcat—why did you?" "Since you insist," she replied, with that pathetic •expression which is a perpetual temptation to rue, I'll telL you. I'm disappointed." "What about?" "Disappointed in you." I could not see Phyllis' fact now. It was hidden in her hands. But her attitude aroused my suspicions. Phyllis is capable even of laughing at me. She looked ;tip quickly, anti my doubts were not allayed. Hereyes were brighter than usual, and the corners of hal-mouth were twitching. " You are not entertaining a bit," she said. Phillis had ire at a disadvantage. Under cover of her long eyelashes she was watching me, and I could not tell whether or no she were serious. "It is the logical outcome of your treatment of rue," I observed, stiffly. "In what way I' " Xiss Vaughan--" Lbegan. Phyllis lips partei in a delicious smile. " I'm seriou" I said. " You are—very."-, " Illusions are the heritage of youth. Itt my case experience has effected a divorce very early. Indeed " (laughingly)—,"and what were your illusions? " " You were one."

1 " Were ? is that nice—or not ? " ' "It's the truth." "So you have no illusions left? " • "None." My tone was charged with tragedy. I wished Phyllis to realise how convinced I was on this point. "If life has little colour now for me, at least, i no disappointments await me." , "It is very sad," Phyllis commented, softly. I could just make out what she said. I dislike , ^ asking people to repeat themselves, especially Phyllis who invariably punishes me by saying something . _ quite different. , nth my armchair. The fauteuil is nice for two. I ' Phyllis either dresoes her hair very high or in a knot, low on the nape of the neck. It was dressed so now-- in a knot—and I think it is the more becom- ing ; though, possibly, I ant prejudiced for the time being. Why Phyllis should have used ribbon of that particular colonr when she has expressed dislike for it (and knowing also that I have a weakness for it) surprises me. But I did not show it; I am sure when she , intercepted my glance I appeared quite incurious, ; though I believe Phyllis was sceptical. 'Toe ribbon in your hair is becoming—impossible to be otherwise—" "Thank you," mockingly, "—But surely you have not noticed the colour ? " # Was ever a more foolish questiou put to a woman? (I have always said that in Phyllis presence I do ~ myself injustice.) I have," she answered. f, Phyllis is the most inconsequent of her sex. ` I was silent. It is the safer course when one is in ; doubt. Phyllis was looking at me—l have caught the ex- pression once or twice beforr—in a manner that ,i bewilders me. , "1t is your own fault," she remarked. "In going t - , out of your way to search for what is not meant, you , make a mistake." Really she herself is to blame for that, not being ,t straigh forward enough. I began to think : Phyllis was aware of the colour , f her ribbon. Phyllis knew I liked it, therefore--! ' "it is good of you," I said, lightly touching the '2 precious trifle—' and Iso undeserving." "If your qualities require depreciating I would rather do it,' reprovingly. .° "I would permit anything from you." "Suppose I promise not to wear the ribbon : again—" "I was desolated. IN SUPPORT OF FREE TRADE.

INTERESTING SPEECH BY MR. A. J. KING.

Protectionist Mis-statements Refuted.

A heavy snow-storm is not conducive to a large attendance at a political meeting, especially in an out of the way village like :Moore, and it was therefore not surprising that the meeting promoted by the Knutsford Division uiberal Association and held in the Church Institute on Monday evening was but sparsely attended. Those who did put in appearance had the advantage of hearing well-reasoned speeches on the chief topics of the day and a criticism of Mr. Chamberlain's Preston speech. The chair was taken by Mr. Josiah Longland and he was supported by Messrs. A. J. King (vice- president of the Knutsford Division Liberal Associa- tion and prospective candidater),C.Middleton (National Morn Club), If. Congreve, C. Roylance, and IV. H. Sherbum. The Chairman was very brief in his remarks. He asked his hearers to bear in mind the approach of t ,e general election and extended a hearty welcome to Mr. King and Mr. Middleton whom they had had the great pleasure of hearing on a previous OCCE44IOII at Moore. Mr. A. J. King moved the following resolution: "That this meeting condemns the Government for its Protectionist propaganda, its financial wastefulness,and its passing of the Education and Licensing Acts against the will of the people." He remarked that if they wive to believe the political prophets— and lne,ior one, could not say that he did— they wer getting very near to the general election. At any rate, t could not be indefinitely delayed unless the Goverumetit did what some of their friends had credited them with the intention of doing, and passed an Act repealing the Septennial Act so that they conld remain in power indefinitely. (Laughter.) It behoved the electors to be making up their minds how they were going to exercise their votes when the election did come. He thought they would agree with him that there had not been for fifty or sixty years a general election fraught with the tremendous issues for the welfare or otherwise of the country that would be brought forward in the impending contest. The present was no time for unsettled convictions. (Hear, hear). Although there was no doubt that the main issue in that division would be on the fiscal queslion— whether we were going to maintain oar Free Trade policy or revert to Protection , under the various names and aspects in which it was lbeing presented—there were many other matters which they could not afford afford to lose sight of or neglect. , They, must, in forming their judgment, look back upon 'the history of the present administration, and decide whether the record justified them giving another lease ,of power to the Government. or whether, as was the 1 opinion in many parts of the division, the tine had come when they should haves change:let the other side have an innings, and see whether they could not manage the affairs of the country a little better than their predecessors. He thought it was perfectly clear that they could not manage them very much worse. (Hear, hear.) He would remind that meeting that the present Government was not the Government which the coun- try retard to power in 1900. The places of many able men who had left the Government had been filled by young " Jacks-in-ollice " who had had no experience of the management of the nation's affairs. The most trustworthy and highly respected members of the Government which was returned in 1900 were now no longer members of it. Dealing with THE EIYUCATION ACT _ . .. .

Mr. King said they had been told that had it not been for the Church of England clergy there would have been up to 1870 practically no education in this country, as the clergy were the only people interested in education and provided the schools. On the face of it there seemed to be a certain amount of truth in the statement, because they knew there were eight or ten thousand parishes in which the only schools for the children to attend were IChurch of England schools. What was the reason? 1 The extreme section of the clergy of the Established Church had never allowed anyone else to start schools. They had always maintained that it was the exclusive privilege of the Church to control the education of the country. Mr. King instanced how when the British and Foreign School Society, which sought to provide good undenominational education, came into existence, the Church party became alarmed and founded the National Society to maintain sciaools!for the education of the children of the poor in the principles of the Church of England. The whole cost of education in the voluntary schools was now paid for out of the public funds and yet the board of management of each school included four denominational managers and only two public repre- tentatives,so that the old principle of public support and public central going together had been violated. In the ease of all non-provided schools the head teachers, who were civil servants, were subjected to religious tests—thus going back to a state of things which they thought had been abolished for ever in this country. Proceeding to deal with the Brussels SUGAR CONVENTION, Mr. King said they were told by all the leading members of the Government that that Convention would not have the effect of rais- ing the price of sugar. As a matter of fact the Convention alone was costing the consumers .of sugar some eight million pounds sterling per annum, apart altogether from the ld. per lb. imposed as a war tax. It was estimated that the increased cost in a workingman's household was about sixpence per week. Before the 'Convention our open market placed us in the position of being able to buy sugar very much cheaper than any other country in the world As a result the enormous confectionery and other industries using sugar as a raw material were established. The Convention had had the effect of increasing the price of sugar, whereas the cost on the continent had gone down. Not only had we imposed a duty on sugar which came from bounty-nay- ins; countrie%, but we had actually pledged ourselves that if it should be the will of a foreign commission on which we hsd only one representative, we would not receive sugar at all from a country which gave a bountv.At the present time we were not allowed to receive sugar from Russia and the Argentine Republic, although our sugar users were anxious for it. The consei ienc4 was that thousands of men who were engaged in sugar- using industries had been

THROWN OUT OF worm, and many thonsands had been put on short time In order to confer a pr daleinati-al benefit upon a few West Indian planters, the MASS of the people in this country had been taxed to the extent of £8,000.000. That was the characteristic of all Tory legislation—the interests of the mass of the people were sacrificed to the interests of a few favoured and influential individuals. (Hear, hear.) The Licensing Act was a piece of legislation on those lines. The measure was closured in such a ruth• less manner that many of the clauses were never dis- cussed in the House of Commons or even explained by the Government. The main clause provided that no licence should in future be refused, without compensa- tion being paid. The Government had converted what was formerly an annual licence into a permanent free- hold, and had put millions of pounds into the pockets of their friends and supporters the 1" trade." The motto of the trade was " our trade our politics," and if we adopted Protection the brewing in- dustry would not be the only trade which followed that motto. All the important trades would combine, and instead of having purity of motive at election time, instead of appealing to what was best in the general in- terests of the country, they would have men sent to IParliament to promote the interests of the special itrades with which they were connected, and they would have legislation to take money out of the pockets of the community and put it into thepockets of a particu- larly favoured class In the case of tied houses the benefit under the new Act would not go to the man who conducted the house, but almost entirely to the brewer. in many cases

- • THE BREWER WOULD BE COMPENSATED TWICE OVER because the closing of houses in congested neighbour- hoods would mean an increased trade to those which were allowed to remain. Mr. King alluded to the national expenditure, pointing out that apart from the South African war the Government had increased the annual expenditure by nearly fifty millions. To his mind the wonder was not that we had so much distress in our large towns, but that we had not a great deal more. Our shipping trade had been bigger than ever. It was Orfu HOME TRADE THAT WAS WRONG simnly because the people had not the money to spend that they ought to have. We diverted 250 millions of capital which would otherwise have been spent in pro- ductive enterprises athome,into the destructive channel of laying waste land in South Africa; we took another fifty millions per annum out of the pockets of the people, and yet we expected them to have as much to spend. The £50,000,000 annual increase was equal to more than 21 far every inhabitant of the country. There was no need to look for any fiscal cause for the distress ; it was due to the extravagant and reckless ex- penditure of the Government. Mr. Chamberlain was speaking at Preston the other day, but he did not seem to get on very happily although he selected the most Conservative town in all Lancashire. He was a very long time in coming to the question of the cotton trade, and when he did reach it, his facts were wrong and his conclusions were erroneous. He told us at Glasgow ttlat cotton was "going," but the cotton trade had been ' very prosperous with the execution that there had been a scarcity of raw material which made Lancashire feel the pinch very badly. Other countries had felt it just as ranch, but the difference was that in Free Trade England we have recovered from the effects very much more quickly than the Pro- tected countries. The

COTTON TRADE was in a booming condition, and last year we exporte a greater length of cotton goods and more pounds o yarn than we had done for many years before. Yet Mr. Chamberlain told the Preston meeting that the increase was merely in the money value. He also quoted figures from the Sine Book showing that in the ten years from 1891 to 1901 the number of people employed in the 'cotton trade had decreased to the extent of 23.700

Mr. Chamberlain omitted to mention the important fact POOR CHILDREN'S TREAT. that there were nearly 20,000 more men employed in the trade now than ten years ago, although the machinery -- —— had so improved that each man could turn out HAPPY SCENE AT THE PARR HALL. a great deal more work. MR. T. J. DOWN'S BANDS. The Future of the Organizations. The people of Warrington will he pleased to hear that there is every probability that the bands which the late Mr. T. J. Down conducted with such marked success will be continued under the leadership of his son Mr. Arthur Down. A practice forthe orchestra has been arranged for February Ist, and on February 2nd, the military band will meet.. We feel sure that in the post of conductor,which his late father so worthily occupied, Mr. Arthur Down will have the enthusiastic support not only of the members of the bands but of the public who have been delighted with the perform- ances in times past. That the organizations will continue to prosper and fulfil their usefuf purpose in the musical life of Warrington will be the general wish of the townspeople. We understand it is the inten- tion that the bands shall retain their old title, so that the honoured name of the late Mr. T. J. Down will always be associated with them.

Independent Order of Rechabites. The annud entertainment and prize distribution of the John Dimond Juvenile tent, took place on Tuesday last in the tent room, Friars Green School. he Superintendent Bro. W. Higginson presided, there being a large number of members and also several o their parents present. A short entertainment consist- ing of recitations and gramaphone selections was given by the juveniles. An entertaining and instruc- tive address on temperance and character was given I by Bro. F. Merlin. Bro. S. Gallop, secretary of the tent, present d his annual report whicn showed that the fortnightly meetings of the tent had been well attended, and work of an interesting and educational character had been performed. The annual picnic to Frodsham in August last was well attended. There were 185 benefitmembers at the 31st December 1903; 119 new members had been admitted during the year, and 3 members transferred to the adult tent,leaving a membership of 201 at December 31st 1904. The tent was also in a Fealthy condition financially. Bro. Edward V. Walker presented the prizes and made a few remarks on the work in which the Rechabites were engaged in training the young to be come members of a sober and industrious nation,l and urged them to take a demo interest both in the tent and the teachings of the order. The following received valuable book prizes : Sisters E. Wright, B. Lewis, M. Lewis, E. Gallop, E. A. Gallop, M. Gallop, L. Gallop, A. Furnival, Alice Furnival, M. Bottles, H. Hill, C. Ball, S. Hewitt, J. H. Norman, J. Middleton, J.R. Clegg. Four prizes were offered to the members for introducing the most new members during the year 1904. First prizes a silver medal and gold brooch (1.0. R. presented by Bro. W. A. Platt, M.8.L., of Berry. The medal was won by Bro. John Ryder, and the gold brooch by Sister S.J. Wilson. The second prizes a silver brooch and scarf pin (I.O.R.),presented by the tent were won by Sister Elsie Jones and Bro. Joseph Hewitt. Two p.C. rulers' sashes were presented by the tent to Bros. W. Yates and H. Fumival for having filled the various offices of the Tent. A vote of thanks pro- posed by Bro. W. Wright, and seconded by Bro. T. Massey, brought to a close one of the most succesful annual meetings of the tent.

CAUTION. A YOUNG WOMAN'S FRAUD.

A young woman, 20 years of age, was recently charged at Nottingham with obtaining no fewer than 8,000 of Watson's soap wrappers by false pretences, and found guiltly. The prosecuting solicitor described the frauds as most impudent. The wrappers from Messrs. Watson's soaps, he said, were valuable, as the firm were giving away over £60,000 in prizes next June, and customers who were saving them were being called upon by a gang of young women, and frandently induced to part with all their wrappers, for twe or three tablets of cheap toilet soap. The defendant had been employed by some woman who had a number of girls going about in different towns, with the result that they were able to make a handsome profit by selling the wrappers again or competing for the money prizes offered by Messrs. Watson. When the detective arrested the prisoner, he discovered thousands of wrappers at her lodgings.

Christianity and the Poverty Problem.

"Christianity and the problem of poverty" was the subject of the Rev. Herbert Dunnico's sermon to a large gathering at the Parr Hall on Sunday evening Taking for his text the words "The poor ye have with you always, and whensoever ye will ye may do them good,"the preacher saidthat 1900 years had passed away since Christ uttered those words, but the problem of poverty in England, at any rate, was not less, but in- finitely greater than it was in the time of our Lord. Still poverty and squalor existed in our Lord's time. He discerned it with unerring eye. He urged its relief. Whenever and wherever He saw it He pitied it. In the spirit of the same Master he appealed to them to con- sider that same problem, but on a greater scale than ever Jesus knew it. If anyone deprecated or criticised the introduction of such a subject into the Christian pulpit,ho would remind them that if Christianity was what it claimed to be it ought to have some light to shed on every problem affecting the lives of men. The preacher said that 30 per cent, of the population of this country were living in poverty, and, surprising though it might be, the appalling fact confronted them that nearly 90 per cont, of the wealth producers of this country had no home they could call their own, they had not a yard of soil they could legally lay claim to, all their belongings could be packed into a decent sized furniture van, and they were so narrowly separated from destitu-ion that a period of depression in trade,sickness or some other similar incident would bring them face to face with hunger and pauperism. For nearly a century the wealth of this country had been increasing at a marvellous rate. The income of this land had risen from 140 millions to 13,000 millions (nearly eight times as much again) whereas the population had increased during that same period hardly half as much again, and yet the strain of life was greater—competition keener, and so vast a percentage of the richest nation in the world were literally starving. Pro- gress and poverty apparently went in hand. Dealing with the causes of poverty, Mr. Dunnico said that it would be well to disabuse their minds at the outset of the idea that the problem was a simple one. It was very complex, more complex than anyone who had not studied it had any idea. The problem of poverty was the inevitable result of the modern social problem, and the modern social problem was largely if not wholly the outcome of the new industrial civiliza- tion. This new civilization had been created by the substitution of mechanical power for physical power. Down to the beginning of last century the world's work was done by muscles, and while muscle was the motive power every man possessed his own. Power then was individualistic, therefore industry was individualistic. The moment the engine became the motive power men then had to gather round it. That meant the factory, division of labour and organiza- tion of industry. Thus whilst the older civilization was individualistic the present was collective, and it was this transformation which had created the present social problem. The speaker referred to the fact that 70 or 80 years ago it was no uncommon thing to find a man who knew at least ten or twelve different trades, men who could do fifty things and do them well; but to-day it took 50 men to do one thing well. He was informed that it took 64 men to-day to make one boot, and each man was dependent upon the other 63; if one stopped they were all stopped. So that whilst under the old regime one man knew a dozen trades, under the new one man knew 1-64th part of one trade. It was the inter-dependence among men which had created the modern social problem and aggravated the problem of poverty. Bearing this in mind, the preacher then said that the cause of poverty was a threefold one and might be stated as follows : (1) insufficient pro- duction ; (2) waste ; (3) unequal distribution. Dealing with the solution of the problem, he said that they must see that no such thing as insufficient produc- tion existed, and that could only be done by utilizing every ounce of the productive power of the nation. The Apostle Paul had said that "if a man will not work neither shall he eat," and when he uttered those words he laid down a fundamental principle of the world. They must, however, bear in mind that there were idle rich as well as idle poor, that there were unemployed at the top of the scale as well as at the bottom. ta his opinion the two were economically related to each other, and if they had not got the one at the top, in all probability they would not have the one at the bottom. Still, whatever they might do to those who would not work (and so far as he was concerned he would make this world a very uncomfortable place for them), there was one principle they must never lose sight of, viz., the right of every man to the product of his own industry. If the com- munity by the complexity of its organization deprived a man of the opportunity to work as he would have done in the old individualistic days, the community was in duty bound to find that manan opening for his labour and to give him a fair return for such labour. If society deprived a man of this opportunity it robbed him of a fundamental right, and if anyone suggested that under the present social order it was not possible to , provide employment for all then the present ' social order was cruel, unjust, evil and the sooner they substituted another social order that would, the better. The next solution he ventured to suggest came under the heading of waste, and he singled out the one he was going to mention because the waste involved was so enormous. They must curtail the drink traffic and reform the public-house. He had studied this question, he had looked at it from every point of view, and it was his candid and unbiased opinion, based upon indisputable facts, that all the causes of poverty put together did not equal this one. They would not, however, solve the problem of the drink traffic by simply reducing the number of licences. For every public-house they closed he would open two, but they would be minus the beer. They must take away from the word public-house all its sinister and evil associations, and make it a house where the working man could comeand enjoy social intercourse, find good reading, and enjoy good Music. They must, however, keep in mind that this wad a question they could solve themselves. Most of them had votes. Let those votes be used wisely and well. There in the very centre of that town they had a magnifinent hall, and perhaps the finest organ in the borough. Both were theirs they owned them, and yet the charges were so exorbitant that no man could hire that hall, engage first-class musicians and vocalists, without being compelled to charge a price beyond the reach of those people who most needed such things. Why did not the Town Council see to it that the public of Warrington had opportunities of using that ball, of listen- ing to good music at a nominal cost? He would tell them why! Because there had been too many publicans and brewers on the Town Council, and too many men who thought about nothing under God's heaven but making money. The next solu- tion, and that had regard to unequal distribution, was to limiter abolish the present competitive system. He wonld be a bold man who would venture to say that competition as we knew it to-day was in any sense of the word Christian. When one SAW the pitiless com- petition of commerce, the unholy struggle to exceed, to take advantage, to over-reach, one was tempted to say that competition was evil and unchristian. He did not venture to say what the future social order would be, but the only social order which could be deemed essenti- ally Christian would be a system under which there would be a more proportionate distribution of the product of industry than at present—a system which would enable every man to feel that he was not fighting for his own good only, but the good of all. He did not say that it would be a system where all men would be equal, but it would be one in which the strong would help the weak, and not the weak the strong. In conclusion, the preacher said that at heart the solution of this great , problem was a religious one. Man was essentially selfish, a change of nature was required to remove this. Law, movements, revolutions could not change that. He (the preacher) knew of only one thing that could, and that was the grace of Him who being rich for our sakes became poor that we through His poverty might be made rich. Poverty of body was a pitiable thing, but poverty of soul was a tragic one. He dreaded poverty. He would not like to die a pauper's death, but he would rather die a pauper than starve his soul and lose his manhood. For what would it profit a man if he should gain the whole world and lose his own soul?
