Farm Fire at Burtonwood.

-- --- The Warrington Fire Brigade had another hard task on Sunday, being engaged during the whole of the day in quelling an outbreak on the premises of Mr. John Rimmer, Ashton's Farm, Burtonwood. The bell was rung at 8.40 in the morning and the brigade, under the direction of the deputy-superintendent (Mr. Frank Turner, jun.). turaed out with great prompti- tude, taking with them the steam engine " Major." On their arrival it was found that a dutch barn, con- taiaing five stacks of hay and corn, was in flames. The weather was bitterly cold, and the fire was fanned by a strong wind. A corner of the adjoining houses had caught fire, but the flames were soon extinguished. The St.Helens fire brigade now came upon the scene,but despite the efforts of the united brigades the barn and contents were destroyed, the damage which is covered by insurance, being estimated at about .:£450. A pig was also roasted alive in its stye. The water supply ; was poor, a brook having to be dammed up. The Warrington brigade returned at five o'clock.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

(By " Ovallst:"/

The display given at Wilderspool on Saturday by Warrington and Hull was one which afforded the crowd immense pleasure. For once in a way we had two teams in opposition who both play open football, and referee who sees that the game is played as it ought to be. To Hr. Renton, almost as much as the players, is credit due for the excellent football shown. There was also the additional gratification for the crowd that the home team won, though the margin was only the narrow one of 13 points against 11 points. Such free scoring is a rarity in Rugby football between teams of equal calibre, but it is a source of undoubted attraction when it does occur. The speed and clever- ness of the men are displayed to much better advan- tage than when they are spoiling one another's efforts and adopting defensive measures.

For once in a way the Warrington defence was helpless. The team with the lowest adverse score in the League was more than a little surprised at having its line crossed three times in one afternoon, but as the enemy were paid back in their own coin the mortification was not great. Still, Hull's feat was no mean one, especially at Wilderspool. The style of play was bound to result in feee scoring, but more than one try on both sides should have been prevented had the defence been steady. Twice the home citadel fell from reasons quite apart from the attack. A charged-down punt brought Hull their first point,and a failure to gather on the part of the full back resulted in the last try of the match, while a somewhat similar error by the Hull custodian gave Warrington their winning lead just after the interval.

When Hull opened the scoring in such 'a business- like fashion, there were some ominous recol- lections of the Leigh match. But Warring- ton were in different form as events soon Proved. They very promptly and smartly sent Hull to the right about and quick work by the halves resulted in D. Davies getting over and Preston goal- ing. Hull were just as quickly back again and before the interval Hall caught the defence napping and slip- ped over:simply, a goal following. Thus at half-time Hull were leading, S-5.

The restart was sensational and reassuring for Warrington. Isherwood smartly collared Jenkins, the Hull full back, and the latter lost possession. Brooks was handy, and showing judgment which older players might have envied he put his toe to the ball and dribbled right under the posts with thc Hull men in hopeless pursuit. Fish kicked a goal,and later on did what everybody had been wanting him to do—he scored a try .This time he missed the goal,and the loss of so easy a point nearly proved costly. Hull scored again, and if Goodfellow had been able to negotiate a difficult shot from touch the match would have been drawn.

An interesting feature of the match was the first appearance at Warrington of Freear, the Irish ex- international wing. When it was found, that he faced Fish excitement ran high. The duel was not, how- ever, very severe between the two. Fish soon proved his mastery over the Hull man, and considering his want of training played a wonderful game. In the first half, he snapped up a pass right under Freear's nose and made a superb, dodgy run almost from goal-line to goal-line which fairly brought down the house, though no score accrued. In the second half he repeatedly got away, and finally did one of his own inimitable dances down the touch-line and round the posts. Freear, though showing evidences of c ever- ness, was not in it with" the irrepressible," and as a Hull man said, it was worth while coming from Hull to see Fish play.

Hockehhall and Brooks came next in order of merit for Warrington. The young centre showed distinct promise, and his taking and giving of passes was much ahead of his previous displays, while the try he got was a beauty. Isherwood was reliable as usual, and little fault could be found with the other. Kenyon is also improving. Hull were well served at half back and centre. Salt, the reserve balf, played a very strong game and would be an acquisition to any club, while Goodfellow and Cook kept the threequarter line moving. The wings and full back were weak at times. Forward, the two teams were well-matched.

It is a pity all games are not up to Saturday's standard. If they were the N.R.U. would soon be the most popular football authority in existence. However it is too much to expect perfection every week. _

The two successive victories since the turn of the year have brought Warrington into the first half-dozen clubs again, and a point ahead of their dear neighbours from Wigan. Bradford, Oldham, and Broughton Rangers all won again and the championship could hardly be a more open question. Runcorn and Batley c.,nfirmed their title to the bottom positions, but Halifax by los- ing at Wakefield have got themselves into serious difficulty. The League table up-to-date is as follows :- -For— —Against— P. W. L. D. G. T. Pts. G. T. Pts Pts. Lancashire completed their cosnty championship matches on Monday when they went down before Cumberland, and proved their consistency for the present season. They have not won a match! On Monday they did not try. There was a regular blizzard at Whitehaven, and the men were nearly frozen stiff. At half-time the Lancashire men were going to abandon the match but resumed under official persuasion. I hear the cold was so intense that the blood from a blow on Bob Wilson's nose froze to his face. In addition the ground was very hard and sore knees were general. Fish, Hockenhall, and Preston represented Warrington, and they don't want another experience like it.

To-day, Warrington have to visit the Bradfordians, who will be burning to avenge the defeat sustained at Warrington on September 24th. They are in the form to do it from all accounts, but they must not expect an easy conquest. Warrington have won at Park Avenue before, and they ought to give as good as they get to-day.

The Warrington team for the match at Bradford is practically tie same as last Saturday, the only change being Naylor for Harmer in the forwards. The fifteen therefore is: Hallam, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Brooks and Kenyon, threequavters ; Flockenhall and D. Davies, half hacks; Boardman, Preston, Shugars, Thomas, Belton, Jolley, Naylor, and W. swift, forwards.

Last season the match at Bradford was an unfortunate one for Warrington, who finished with thirteen men and were defeated by 14 points to nil. Harris got his collar bone broken in the first five minutes, and Isherwood sustained a severe injury to the lea, a minute or two later. With better luck to day the team should give a better account of themselves.

Ashton, the youngest brother of the popular secretary, while there will be once more two Bates operating at the base of the pack. The "A. N. Other will probably be W. Bate, a younger brother of the famous "Smack," who has for some time been on the St. Helens register. In the forwards a new man, named Owens, from the Brewery team, is being given a trial.

WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL.

j The Walton :Lay-by Used.

The useful purpose which the lay-by on the Manchester Ship Canal at Walton might serve in facilitating local commerce has received a practical demonstration this week, a cargo having been taker, aboard a coasting steamer in a remarkably short space of time. The steamship "Watchful" belonging to Messrs. F. H. Powell and Co., Manchester, left Manchester for Bristol and Plymouth on Wednesday evening. The bridgemaster at Stag Bridge was duly advised by telephone from Mode Wheel Locks, and reported by instructions from Captain Waring (Canal Superintendent at Latchford) to the Borate Company and the lockmaster at Walton that the" Watchful" was waiting to pass through the Latchford locks at 11 p.m. She arrived off the lay-by at 11.55 and was made fast at 12.5. The cargo of four tons of borate of soda was put on board in ten minutes and the vessel was clear of her moorings at 12-15 ;so that between the time she came in sight and her departure from the lay-by only twenty minutes elapsed. As the works of the Borate Company are kept going all the night and in addition there are two men on night duty in charge of the locks, it has been demon- strated that there is no difficulty in handling steamers by night. Everything was done with clockwork prod- sion,and we understand that the captain of the" Watchful" was very pleased with the rapidity with which the vessel was moored and the cargo placed aboard. The goods, which were in sacks, were run down a short straight into the hold of the vessel. They had been previously neatly piled by the employees of the Borate Company on the dolphin (a projection froal the actual quay) wlioh enables ocean-going steamers to come alongside, so that in some instances goods can be trucked right on to the deck, without use of crane. The projecting dolphins also permit of two barges being alongside a steamer, one on the port and the other on the larboard This arrangement enables four operations to be carried on at the same time, viz. : goods can be loaded into barges, in railway waggons by transporter, lifted off by ship's winch, and trucked off. Adjoining the lay-by is the gridiron, permitting the marshalling of four trains at one time, whilst there is an excellent level road for carts and motor traffic. The borate cargo, if not taken off by the "Watchful, would have gone to Liverpool and been shipped fro= that port. Many thousands of tons of goods areannnally barged or trained to Liverpool by Warrington traders, t because the direct facilities available by means of the ' lay-by are either unknown or unappreciated. Warrington traders requiring steamers to stop at the Warrington lay-by should communicate at once with Alderman Arthur Bennett, the secretary of the Chamber of Commerce. Freight can be arranged direct with the Manchester office of the various shipping companies. Customs entry can be made to the customs officer, Customs House, Runcorn (telephone No. 3 Runcorn). Goods can be delivered on the quay by London and North-Western Railway, by motor, or cart, which ever is most convenient. Porterage can be arranged with the Canal Company. The Manchester addresses of steamship owners passing Warrington appeared in last week's "Examiner.

Monks, Hall and Co.'s Social. The managers and office staff of this firm, together with friends, held their first social on Friday evening last in the Masonic Rooms, which were suitably de- corated for the occasion. The gathering numbered over SO, and the programme was arranged to suit the various tastes of the party, dancing, progressive whist and other games taking place simultaneously. During the evening a letter was read from Mr. F. Monks, J.P., regretting his inability to be present owing to extreme pressure of business. Mr. Poole favoured the company at intervals with songs, which were excellently ren- dered, and highly appreciated. Mr. A. Wright's band supplied the music for dancing, and gave every satisfaction, whilst the catering was in the capable hands of Mrs. Atkinson. The proceedings terminated shortly after. midnight with a vote of thanks to the stewards and Mr. F. Dumbill, who acted as secretary, and as the function passed off so successfully, the opinion was freely expressed that the social should become an annual one.

Deaths. HARTLEY.—On the 20th inst. at The Limes; Grappen- hall-road, Stockton Heath, Warrington, Herbert Nicholson (Bertie), aged 17 years, the youngest son of J. N. Hartley, Collector of Inland Revenue, Warrington. In Memoriam. In ever-loving memory of my dear mother, Hannah Owen, who fell asleep January 17th, 1897. Aged 70 years. " Gone, but never forgotten."

road, Sutton; John Eden (15), Derbyshire-hill ; James Bothwell (17), Fairclough-street, Burtonwood ; David Bothwell (21), Junction-lane, Sutton. Among the injured the most serious case is thaltiof young Bradshaw, as it is feared he is suffering Mina internal injuries as well as a fractured thigh. John Eden has also a broken thigh, and others are suffering from crushed legs and ribs, sprained knees and ankles, cuts and bruises. The medical staff at the hospital hope, however, that there will be no further death. At the pithead the ascending cage was checked by a patent disconnecter, but the heavy wire rope was re leased, and swung over the pulley on to the roof of the engine-house, The roof was cut through, and part of the front wall of the building torn down. The engine, however, escaped injury. THE INQUEST OPENED. THE ENGINE WINDER NOT PRESENT. The inquiry into the deplorable catastrophe which occurred on Monday morning at the Bold Colliery, near St. Helens Junction, was opened by Mr. Brighouse. at the Clock Face Inn, on the road between St. Helens and Warrington on Tuesday evening. The colliery company was represented by Mr. Edwin Peace (Messrs. Peace and Darlington) ; Mr. J. G Thompson general manager ; and Mr. W. Southern secretary of the company. Mr. Pennington Riley appeared for the relatives of John Swift. Mr. Peace said he appeared for the Collins Green Colliery Company. The Cononer: Do you appear for the winder as well? Mr. Peace: No. The Coroner : _ls he here? Mr. Peace : Ido not know. The Coroner : Officer, where is the engine-winder? Is he here ? Sergeant Turner: Ido not know. The Coroner: I should have thought he would have been the most important person to be here, and a per.. son to whom it is of the first importance to be here Have you warned him? Sergeant Turner: I do not know if the colliery company have warned him, but we could not find him. Mr. Thompson have you warned him. Mr. Thompson: No, I have not seen him? The Coroner: What is the name of the winder? Sergeant Turner : James Fowler. We got the address, 2, Brunswick-road, Earlestown. bat I have not seen him since the accident. The Coroner: I should think it would be very use- ful indeed to know where he is.

Diagram 51 shows the two object balls close to the side cushion, the player being in hand. The ordinary player plays the cannon by taking the first ball (in the diagram this is the red) either half-ball or finer than half-ball. Such a stroke) must inevitably leave the red ball behind the cue ball, and the stroke would have to be ' very %Tell played to leave the cue ball and object white well placed for a centre pocket in-off. The correct 'ray of playing the cannon is by a run- through stroke. The cue ball should be placed at or near the end of the D, and struck with plenty of right hand side, and aim should be taken nearly full on the object ball. By this means the red will be driven across the table, and in many positions it will be possible to send the red to the vicinity of the centre pocket. The right hand side on the cue ball will be running side off the cushion, and its effect will be strongly shown in making the cannon. The exact position of the object balls after the stroke has been played cannot of course be exactly de- fined, brut the red may be in position for a pot or an in-off, an in-off from the white may also be left, and often aa easy cannon will be on owing to the object balls by this run-through stroke having been left in front of the cue ball. In playing this cannon care must be taken to avoid a kiss which in some positions can easily happen should the object ball be taken a little fuller than the making of the stroke demands. DIAGRAM 52 • - • .06 • I 4 1011. - S. A.two-cushion run-through cannon. Red ball 394 inches from the top cushion and 24 incli.,?s from the side cushion, object white 17 inches from the side cushion and lj inches from the top cushion, and cue bail on a line between the centre pockets and 6 inches from the centre spot.

Diagram 52 shows another run-through cannon. This cannon belongs to the same class as the one ihown on Diagram 51, the difference, however, being that in this case there is not the alternative of a simple ball-to-ball cannon. In playing this two-cushion run-through cannon, the cue-ball should be struck above the centre and with plenty of running side (left-hand side in the dia- gram). If the strength be well judged, good posi- tion will be left for the next stroke owing to the red being doubled across the t.,ble to the vicinity of the corner pocket, as slat-. -n by the intersected line on the diagram. ~ DIAGRAM 53 ... ....wans-,, , jilatiminommummummgmammow 'll 1 i•. . notning but a pot and the red ball be quite safe. Of course, the whittle might run nicely te leave an in-off, but to play the cannon in this way is always to court disaster. The high class way of playing the cannon is by making the stroke a run-through (spotting the cue ball accordingly). Played in this way the cue ball has a lot of pace taken out of it by the fuller co..!act it makes with the red, and conse- quently there is little or no fear of the white ball being potted or driven rig!' on to the brink of the pocket, and look at Le different path given to the red by the run-t::1 eugh stroke! In- stead of being left in the rear of the cue-ball, as it must be by an ordinary hall-to-ball stroke, the red is driven into the upper part of the table, and thus far more latitude is given for good position for the m-ct stroke. A well played stroke may leave the red nicely sithated for a pot into the corner pocket (the thick intersected line on the diagram shows the direction that the fuller con- tact may give the red ball, and the thin hue TllO path the red ball may bake as the result of a half-ball contact). Or an in-off may be left instead of a pot, and often an easy cannon from the white on to the red may also be left. In addition there will always be the chance of position being left for an in-off from the white. This run-through cannon is, however, by no means an easy stroke, and the player who has never exceeded a 30 break would almost cer- tainly fail at it if he tried it in the course of a game. Pr- .ty good players should, however, always ph this cannon as a run-through when the white is the second object ball. It can hardly need emphasising that it is far better to play strokes in the correct way, and to miss scoring from them occasionally than to stick like glue to the old, old wrong way and never improve one's game. it must be said that, on the day's play the better tea= lost,for whereas the whole of Latchford team played a bustling,fast game,and the home forwards were quite at their worst, the Latchford half backs breaking up any attempt at combination with ease. The Latchford team are a well balancikl side, and have in their ranks some players of more than average merit. Among their halves is Wilkinson, who proved a great stumbling block to the home forwards, and I should place him among the best halves, I have seen this year, while Rathbone if not so brilliant, played a good sound game. in the forwards is Banks, a forward whom I have always Ldmired for his dash and gentlemanly play, and last week he was certainly the best forward on the field. J. Spensley and Cliffe made a good wing being very fast, and with a little more understanding among the quinttete, the Latchford team would win a lot of matches. R.Hallows was the best of the two backs,ancl I. Wilkinson in goal could not be blamed for the shot that scored. --- The display given by Stockton Heath was very dis- appointing, although it must be taken into considera- tion, that Eric Fairclough got his" specs," broken at the commencement, and for the rest of the game could not see the ball properly, which gave the Latchford forwards more room to move about in, than would otherwise have been the case, besides weakening the attacking power of the home team. -- -- None of the home forwards seemed to be able to judge the speed of the ball, but this might be caused by the team having played so few home matches, and having played the last two Saturday's on laid grounds, Sale and Timperley. The goal for Stockton Heath was scored by R. Pierpoint. J. Richardson and L. Fairclough played well at half, and E. Pierpoint and E. E. Woods at back were most effective, and to these four players the chief honours of a lucky victory must go. I The Latchford second entertained the Stockton Heath second at Latchford, and won by 2-0. The score about represents the play, although it should be mentioned that one of the visiting team failed to put in an appear. ance. --- Last week's matches caused some surprises, for instance Bowdon beat Alderley Edge by 6-0, while Brooklands overcame Kersal. Sale only defeated Moss Side by 2-0, the score being kept low, by the excellent form shown by the Moss Side goal keeper. West Derby and Western, two old clubs who have struck a bad patch, played a draw, though Western had a more scratchy team than usual, and had only ten men during the whole game.
